Nor do we need to have recourse to the fictions of poets to learn this; but besides the reasoning of the matter, we may discover the same truth by common experience and observation. It is easy to remark that a cordial affection renders all things common among friends; and that married people in particular mutually lose their property, and are unacquainted with the *mine* and *thine*, which are so necessary, and yet cause such disturbance in human society. The same effect arises from any alteration in the circumstances of mankind; as when there is such a plenty of anything as satisfies all the desires of men: In which case the distinction of property is entirely lost, and everything remains in common. This we may observe with regard to air and water, though they are the most valuable of all external objects; and we may easily conclude that if men were supplied with everything in the same abundance, or if *everyone* had the same affection and tender regard for *everyone* as for himself; justice and injustice would be equally unknown among mankind.

---
*Of the origin of justice and property.*

**HERE** then is a proposition which, I think, may be regarded as certain: that it is only from the selfishness and confined generosity of men, along with the scanty provision nature has made for their wants, that justice derives its origin. If we look backward, we shall find that this proposition bestows additional force on some of those observations which we have already made on this subject.

*First*, we may conclude from it that a regard for the public interest, or a strong and extensive benevolence, is not our first and original motive for observing the rules of justice; since it is allowed that if men were endowed with such benevolence, these rules would never have been dreamed of.

*Secondly*, we may conclude from the same principle that the sense of justice is not founded on reason, or on the discovery of certain connections and relations of ideas which are eternal, immutable, and universally obligatory. For since it is confessed that such an alteration as that mentioned above, in the temper and circumstances of mankind, would entirely alter our duties and obligations, it is necessary—according to the common system that the *sense of virtue is derived from reason*—to show the

But it is evident that the only cause why the extensive generosity of man and the perfect abundance of everything would destroy the very idea of justice is because they render it useless; and that, on the other hand, his confined benevolence and his needy condition give rise to that virtue only by making it necessary to the public interest and to that of every individual. It was, therefore, a concern for our own and the public interest which made us establish the laws of justice; and nothing can be more certain than that it is not any relation of ideas which gives us this concern, but our impressions and sentiments, without which everything in nature is perfectly indifferent to us and can never in the least affect us. The sense of justice, therefore, is not founded on our ideas, but on our impressions.

*Thirdly, we may further confirm the previous proposition: that those impressions which give rise to this sense of justice are not natural to the mind of man, but arise from artifice and human conventions.* For since any considerable alteration of temperament and circumstances equally destroys justice and injustice, and since such an alteration has an

But if men pursued the public interest naturally and with a hearty affection, they would never have dreamed of restraining each other by these rules; and if they pursued their own interest without any precaution, they would run headlong into every kind of injustice and violence. These rules, therefore, are artificial and seek their end in an oblique and indirect manner; nor is the interest which gives rise to them of a kind that could be pursued by the natural and inartificial passions of men.

To make this more evident, consider that though the rules of justice are established merely by interest, their connection with interest is somewhat singular and is different from what may be observed on other occasions. A single act of justice is frequently contrary to *public interest*; and were it to stand alone, without being followed by other acts, it may, in itself, be very prejudicial to society. When a man of merit, of a beneficent disposition, restores a great fortune to a miser or a seditious bigot, he has acted justly and laudably, but the public is a real sufferer. Nor is

<margin>SECT. II. *Of the origin of justice and property.*</margin>
may be the consequence of any single act of justice, performed by a single person, yet the whole system of actions, participated in by the whole society, is infinitely advantageous to the whole and to every part; it is not long before justice and property are established. Every member of society is aware of this interest: everyone expresses this awareness to his fellows, along with the resolution he has taken to align his actions with it, on the condition that others will do the same. Nothing more is required to induce anyone who has the first opportunity to perform an act of justice. This becomes an example to others. And thus justice establishes itself by a kind of convention or agreement; that is, by a sense of interest supposed to be common to all, where every single act is performed in the expectation that others are to perform the same. Without such a convention, no one would ever have dreamed that there was such a virtue as justice, or have been induced to conform his actions to it. Considering any single act, my justice may be harmful in every respect; and it is only upon the assumption that others are to imitate my example that I can be induced to embrace that virtue, since nothing but this combination can render justice advantageous,

He argues that while individual acts of justice might be harmful in isolation, the collective system of mutual expectation makes the virtue of justice beneficial to everyone.</summary>

WE come now to the *second* question we proposed, *namely: Why we attach the idea of virtue to justice, and of vice to injustice.* This question will not detain us long after the principles which we have already established. All we can say of it at present will be dispatched in a few words: and for further satisfaction, the reader must wait until we come to the *third* part of this book. The *natural* obligation to justice, *namely* interest, has been fully explained; but as to the *moral* obligation, or the sentiment of right and wrong, it will first be necessary to examine the natural virtues before we can give a full and satisfactory account of it.

AFTER men have found by experience that their selfishness and limited generosity, acting freely, totally incapacitate them for society; and at the same time have observed that society is necessary to the satisfaction of those very passions, they are naturally induced to lay themselves under the restraint of such rules as may render their interaction more safe and convenient. ^ This latter principle of Sympathy is too weak to control our passions; but has sufficient force to influence our Taste, and give us the Sentiments of Approbation or Blame.

Though this progress of the sentiments be natural, and even necessary, it is certain that it is here forwarded by the artifice of politicians, who, in order to govern men more easily and preserve peace in human society, have endeavored to produce an esteem for justice and an abhorrence of injustice. This, no doubt, must have its effect; but nothing can be more evident than that the matter has been carried too far by certain writers on morals, who seem to have employed their utmost efforts to extirpate all sense of virtue from among mankind. Any artifice of politicians may assist nature in the producing of those sentiments which she suggests to us, and may even on some occasions produce alone an approbation or esteem for any particular action; but it is impossible it should be the sole cause of the distinction we make between vice and virtue.

For if nature did not aid us in this particular, it would be in vain for politicians to talk of *honorable* or *dishonorable*, *praiseworthy* or *blameworthy*. These words would be perfectly unintelligible and would no more have any idea annexed to them than if they were from a language perfectly unknown to us. The utmost politicians can achieve is to extend the natural sentiments beyond their original bounds; but nature must still furnish the materials and give us some notion of moral distinctions.

Just as public praise and blame increase our esteem for justice, so private education and instruction contribute to the same effect. For as parents easily observe that a man is more useful, both to himself and others, the greater the degree of probity and honor he is endowed with, and that those principles have greater force when custom and education assist interest and reflection: for these reasons, they are induced to instill in their children, from their earliest infancy, the principles of probity and teach them to regard the observance of those rules by which society is maintained as worthy and honorable, and their violation as base and infamous. By this means, the sentiments of honor may take root in their tender minds,

WHAT further contributes to increase their solidity is the interest of our reputation, once the opinion *that merit or demerit accompanies justice or injustice* is firmly established among mankind. There is nothing that affects us more closely than our reputation, and nothing on which our reputation more depends than our conduct regarding the property of others. For this reason, everyone who has any regard for their character, or who intends to live on good terms with mankind, must establish an inviolable law for themselves: never, by any temptation, to be induced to violate those principles which are essential to a person of probity and honor.

I SHALL make only one observation before I leave this subject: namely, that although I assert that in the *state of nature*, or that imaginary state which preceded society, there is neither justice nor injustice, I do not assert that it was allowable in such a state to violate the property of others. I only maintain that there was no such thing as property, and consequently there could be no such thing

## SECTION III.
### *Of the rules which determine property.*

<margin>SECTION III.</margin>
Although the establishment of the rule concerning the stability of possession is not only useful, but even absolutely necessary to human society, it can never serve any purpose while it remains in such general terms. Some method must be shown by which we may distinguish what particular goods are to be assigned to each particular person, while the rest of mankind are excluded from their possession and enjoyment. Our next business, then, must be to discover the reasons which modify this general rule and fit it to the common use and practice of the world.

## II.

<margin>Of justice and injustice.</margin>
either the *particular* person or the public may reap from his enjoyment of any *particular* goods, beyond what would result from the possession of them by any other person.

It would be better, no doubt, if everyone were possessed of what is most suitable to him and proper for his use. But besides the fact that this relation of fitness may be common to several people at once, it is liable to so many controversies—and men are so partial and passionate in judging these controversies—that such a loose and uncertain rule would be absolutely incompatible with the peace of human society. The convention concerning the stability of possession is entered into in order to cut off all occasions of discord and contention; and this end would never be attained were we allowed to apply this rule differently in every particular case, according to every particular utility which might be discovered in such an application. Justice, in her decisions, never regards the fitness or unfitness of objects to particular persons, but conducts herself by more extensive views. Whether a man is generous or a miser, he is equally well-received by her, and obtains with the same ease a decision in his favor, even for what is entirely useless to him.

<vocab>Justice, Injustice, stability of possession, fitness, human society, utility, property</vocab>

<summary>Hume argues that property rights must be governed by general rules rather than individual "fitness" or "utility," because judging ownership based on personal suitability would cause constant social discord. He emphasizes that justice must remain impartial, granting possession regardless of whether the owner is a miser or a generous person.</summary>

SECTION

---
*Of the rules, which determine property.*
</margin>

It follows, therefore, that the general rule, *that possession must be stable*, is not applied by particular judgments, but by other general rules, which must extend to the whole society, and be inflexible to either spite or favor. To illustrate this, I propose the following instance. I first consider men in their savage and solitary condition; and suppose, that being aware of the misery of that state, and foreseeing the advantages that would result from society, they seek each other's company, and make an offer of mutual protection and assistance. I also suppose, that they are endowed with such sagacity as to immediately perceive that the chief impediment to this project of society and partnership lies in the greed and selfishness of their natural temper; to remedy which, they enter into a convention for the stability of possession, and for mutual restraint and forbearance. I am aware that this method of proceeding is not altogether natural; but besides the fact that I here only suppose those reflections to be formed all at once, which in fact arise insensibly and by degrees; besides this, I say, it is very possible that several persons, being by different accidents separated from the societies to which they formerly belonged, may be obliged to form a new society

It is evident, then, that their first difficulty, in this situation, after the general convention for the establishment of society and for the stability of possession, is how to separate their possessions and assign to each his particular portion, which he must for the future unalterably enjoy. This difficulty will not detain them long; but it must immediately occur <gloss>to them</gloss> as the most natural expedient, that *everyone continue to enjoy what he is at present master of, and that property or constant possession be conjoined to immediate possession.* Such is the effect of custom, that it not only reconciles us to anything we have long enjoyed, but even gives us an affection for it, and makes us prefer it to other objects, which may be more valuable, but are less known to us. What has long lain under our eye, and has often been employed to our advantage, *that* we are always the most unwilling to part with; but we can easily live without possessions which we have never enjoyed, and are not accustomed to. It is evident, therefore, that men would easily acquiesce in this expedient, *that everyone continue to enjoy what he is at present possessed of;*

<sup>a</sup> <note>No questions in philosophy are more difficult than, when a number of causes present themselves for the same phenomenon, to determine which is the principal and predominant one. There is seldom any very precise argument to fix our choice, and men must be content to be guided by a kind of taste or fancy, arising from analogy and a comparison of similar instances. Thus, in the present case, there are, no doubt, motives of public interest for most of the rules which determine property; but still I suspect that these rules are principally fixed by the imagination, or the more frivolous properties of our thought and conception. I shall continue to explain these causes, leaving it to the reader’s choice whether he will prefer those derived from public utility or those derived from the imagination. We shall begin with the right of the present possessor.
It is a quality which (<i>a</i>) I have already observed in human nature, that when two objects appear in a close relation to each other, the mind is apt to ascribe to them any additional relation in order to complete the union; and this inclination is so strong as often to make us run into errors (such as that of the conjunction of thought and matter) if we find that they can serve that purpose. Many of our impressions are incapable of place or local position; and yet those very impressions we suppose to have a local conjunction with the impressions of sight and touch, merely because they are conjoined by causation and are already united in the imagination. Since, therefore, we can feign a new relation, and even an absurd one, in order to complete any union, it will easily be imagined that if there are any relations which depend on the mind, it will readily conjoin them to any preceding relation and unite, by a new bond, such objects as already have a union in the fancy. And as property forms a relation between a person and an object, it is natural to found it on some preceding relation; and as property is nothing but a constant possession, secured by the laws of society, it is natural to add it to the present possession, which is a relation that resembles it. For this also has its influence. If it be natural to conjoin all sorts of relations, it is even more so to conjoin such relations as are resembling and are related together.

### SECTION III.
---

<margin>Regarding the rules that determine property.</margin>
The possession of all external goods is changeable and uncertain; this is one of the most significant obstacles to the establishment of society and is the reason why, by universal agreement—whether express or tacit—men restrain themselves by what we now call the rules of justice and equity. The misery of the condition that precedes this restraint is the reason why we submit to that remedy as quickly as possible; and this provides <note>the word 'us' is struck through</note> an easy reason why we attach the idea of property to the first possession, or to *occupation*. Men are unwilling to leave property in suspense, even for the shortest time, or to open the slightest door to violence and disorder. To this we may add that the first possession always captures the attention most; and if we were to neglect it, there would be no plausible reason for assigning property to any subsequent possession<sup>b</sup>.

<margin>
**PART II.**
Of justice and injustice.
</margin>

THERE remains nothing but to determine exactly what is meant by possession; and this is not as easy as may at first sight be imagined. We are said to be in possession of anything, not only when we immediately touch it, but also when we are so situated with respect to it as to have it in our power to use it; and we may move, alter, or destroy it, according to our present pleasure or advantage. This relation, then, is a species of cause and effect; and as property is nothing but a stable possession, derived from the rules of justice or the conventions of men, it is to be considered as the same species of relation. But here we may observe that as the power of using any object becomes more or less certain, according to whether the interruptions we may meet with are more or less probable; and as this probability may increase by insensible degrees; it is in many cases impossible to determine when possession begins or ends; nor is there any certain standard by which we can decide such controversies. A wild boar that falls into our snares is deemed to be in our possession if

---

but in a figurative sense. Properly speaking, we only make an alteration on it by our labor. This forms a relation between us and the object; and from that arises the property, according to the preceding principles.

<summary>Hume defines possession as a relationship of cause and effect based on one's power to use or alter an object. He notes that because the certainty of this power varies by degrees, it is often impossible to establish a fixed standard for when possession begins or ends.</summary>

<note>c</note> If we seek a solution to these difficulties in reason and public interest, we will never find satisfaction; and if we look for it in the imagination, it is evident that the qualities which operate upon that faculty run so insensibly and gradually into each other that it is impossible to give them any precise bounds or limits. The difficulties on this subject must increase when we consider that our judgment alters very noticeably according to the subject, and that the same power and proximity will be deemed possession in one case which is not esteemed as such in another. A person who has hunted a hare to the point of total exhaustion would look upon it as an injustice for another to rush in before him and seize his prey. But the same person, advancing to pluck an apple that hangs within his reach, has no reason to complain if another, more alert, passes him and takes possession. What is the reason for this difference, except that immobility—not being natural to the hare, but rather the effect of industry—forms in that case a strong relation with the hunter, which is lacking in the other?

Here, then, it appears that a certain and infallible power of enjoyment, without touch or some other perceptible relation, often does not produce property. And I further observe that a perceptible relation, without any present power, is sometimes sufficient to give a title to any object. Seeing a thing is seldom considered a significant relation and is only regarded as such when the object is hidden or very obscure; in which case we find that the view alone conveys a property, according to the maxim *that even a whole continent belongs to the nation that first discovered it.* It is, however, remarkable that

But such disputes may arise not only concerning the real existence of property and possession, but also concerning their extent; and these disputes are often susceptible to no decision, or can be decided by no other faculty than the imagination. A person who lands on the shore of a small island that is desert and uncultivated is deemed its possessor from the very first moment and acquires the

> that both in the case of discovery and that of possession, the first discoverer and possessor must join to the relation an intention of making himself the proprietor; otherwise, the relation will not have its effect. This is because the connection in our imagination between the property and the relation is not so great that it does not require help from such an intention.

From all these circumstances, it is easy to see how perplexed many questions may become concerning the acquisition of property by occupation; even the slightest effort of thought may present us with instances that are not susceptible to any reasonable decision. If we prefer real examples over fictional ones, we may consider the following case, which is found in almost every writer who has treated the laws of nature. Two *Grecian* colonies, leaving their native country in search of new homes, were informed that a city near them was deserted by its inhabitants. To determine the truth of this report, they immediately dispatched two messengers, one from each colony. Finding upon their approach that the information was true, the messengers began a race together with the intention of taking possession of the city, each for his own countrymen. One of these messengers, realizing he was not an equal match for the other, launched his spear at the gates of the city and was fortunate enough to fix it there before the arrival of his companion. This produced a dispute between the two colonies over which of them was the proprietor of the empty city, and this dispute still persists among philosophers. For my part, I find the dispute impossible to decide, because the whole question depends upon the imagination, which in this case possesses no precise or determinate standard upon which it can give sen-

<margin>
PART II.
Of justice and injustice.

The nature of human society does not admit of any great accuracy; nor can we always go back to the first origin of things in order to determine their present condition. Any considerable space of time sets objects at such a distance that they seem, in a way, to lose their reality, and have as little influence on the mind as if they had never existed. A man’s title, which is clear and certain at present, will seem obscure and doubtful fifty years from now, even though the facts on which it is founded should be proved with the greatest evidence and certainty. The same facts do not have the same influence after so long an interval of time. And this may be received as a convincing argument for our preceding doctrine with regard to property and justice. Possession during a long period of time conveys a title to any object. But as it is certain that, although everything is produced in time, there is nothing real that is produced by time; it follows that property, being produced by time, is not anything real in the objects, but is the offspring of the sentiments, on which alone time is found to have any influence <note>Present possession is plainly a relation between a person and an object; but is not sufficient to counterbalance the relation of first possession, unless the former is long and uninterrupted: In which case the relation is increased on the side of the</note>.

<margin>
SECT. III.
~~~
Of the rules that determine property.
</margin>

WE acquire ownership of objects by *accession* when they are closely connected to objects we already own, while also being subordinate to them. Thus, the fruits of our garden, the offspring of our livestock, and the work of our slaves are all considered our property, even before we take possession of them. When objects are linked together in the imagination, they tend to be treated the same way and are commonly assumed to share the same qualities. We easily move from thinking about one to the other and make no distinction in our judgments of them, especially if the latter object is subordinate to the former <gloss>c</gloss>.

<meta>catchword: THE</meta>

---

<gloss>c</gloss> This source of property can only be explained by the imagination; one may affirm that the causes here are uncomplicated. We will proceed to explain them more specifically and illustrate them with examples from everyday life and experience.

It was noted above that the mind has a natural tendency to join related things, especially those that resemble each other, and finds a sense of fitness and consistency in such a union. From this tendency are derived these laws of nature: *that at the initial formation of society, property always follows current possession; and afterward, it arises from first possession or long-term possession.* Now we can easily observe that a relationship is not limited to just one degree. From an object related to us, we acquire a relationship to every other object related to it, and so on, until the mind loses the chain because the progression is too long. However, even if the relationship might

The right of *succession* is a very natural one, arising from the presumed consent of the parent or near relation, and from the general interest of mankind, which requires that men’s

weakens by each degree of removal, it is not immediately destroyed, but frequently connects two objects by means of an intermediate one related to both. And this principle is so powerful that it gives rise to the right of *accession* and causes us to acquire property not only in the objects we immediately possess, but also in those closely connected with them.

Suppose a *German*, a *Frenchman*, and a *Spaniard* enter a room where three bottles of wine—*Rhenish*, *Burgundy*, and *Port*—are placed on the table. Suppose they begin to quarrel over how to divide them; an umpire chosen to settle the dispute would naturally, to show his impartiality, give each person the product of his own country. This follows from a principle that is, to some extent, the source of those laws of nature that assign property through occupation, prescription, and accession.

In all these cases, and particularly that of accession, there is first a *natural* union between the idea of the person and that of the object, followed by a new and *moral* union produced by the right or property we attribute to that person. But here a difficulty arises that deserves our attention and may give us an opportunity to test the unusual method of reasoning used on this subject. I have already observed that the imagination moves more easily from small to great than from great to small, and that the transition of ideas is always easier and smoother in the former case than in the latter. Now, since the right of accession arises from the easy transition of ideas by which related objects are connected, it would naturally be expected that the right of accession would increase in strength in proportion to the ease of that transition. It might be thought, therefore, that when we have acquired ownership of a small object, we would readily consider any large object related to it as an accession belonging to the owner of the small one. This is because the transition from a small object to a large one is very easy and should connect them in the closest way. But in fact, the opposite is always found to

be otherwise. The empire of *Great Britain* seems to draw along with it the dominion of the *Orkneys*, the *Hebrides*, the Isle of *Man*, and the Isle of *Wight*; but the authority over those lesser islands does not naturally imply any title to *Great Britain*. In short, a small object naturally follows a large one as its accession; but a large one is never supposed to belong to the proprietor of a small one related to it, merely on account of that property and relation. Yet in this latter case, the transition of ideas is smoother from the proprietor to the small object, which is his property, and from the small object to the large one, than in the former case from the proprietor to the large object, and from the large one to the small. It may therefore be thought that these phenomena are objections to the previous hypothesis, *that assigning property to accession is nothing but an effect of the relations of ideas, and of the smooth transition of the imagination.*

It will be easy to solve this objection if we consider the agility and unsteadiness of the imagination, along with the different views in which it is continually placing its objects. When we attribute to a person a property in two objects, we do not always pass from the person to one object, and from that to the other related to it. Since the objects are to be considered here as the property of the person, we are inclined to join them together and place them in the same light. Suppose, therefore, a large and a small object to be related together; if a person is strongly related to the large object, he will likewise be strongly related to both objects, considered together, because he is related to the most significant part. On the contrary, if he is only related to the small object, he will not be strongly related to both, considered together, since his relation lies only with the most trivial part, which is not likely to strike us to any great degree when we consider the whole. And this is the reason why small objects become accessions to large ones, and not large ones to small.

It is the general opinion of philosophers and <term>civilians</term> <note>Scholars of civil law.</note> that the sea is incapable of becoming the property of any nation; and that is because it is impossible to take possession of it, or form any such distinct relation with it as may be the foundation of property.

<summary>Hume explains that accession typically assigns property of a smaller object to the owner of a larger, related one because the imagination groups them based on the most significant part. He also notes that the sea cannot be private property because it cannot be possessed or uniquely related to a single owner.</summary>
<keywords>property, accession, imagination, relation, succession, Great Britain, maritime law, civilians</keywords>

property. Where this reasoning ceases, property immediately takes place. Thus, the most strenuous advocates for the freedom of the seas universally allow that firths and bays naturally belong as an accession to the owners of the surrounding continent. These properly have no more bond or union with the land than the *Pacific* Ocean would have; but having a union in the imagination, and being at the same time *inferior*, they are of course regarded as an accession.

The property of rivers, by the laws of most nations and by the natural turn of our thought, is attributed to the owners of their banks, except for such vast rivers as the *Rhine* or the *Danube*, which seem too large for the imagination to follow as an accession to the property of the neighboring fields. Yet even these rivers are considered the property of that nation through whose dominions they run; the idea of a nation being of a suitable size to correspond with them and bear such a relation to them in the imagination.

<margin>they</margin>
The accessions that are made to lands bordering upon rivers follow the land, say the civil lawyers, provided they are made by what they call *alluvion*—that is, insensibly and imperceptibly. These are circumstances that greatly assist the imagination in this connection. Where there is any considerable portion torn at once from one bank and joined to another, it does not become the property of the person whose land it falls on until it unites with the land, and until the trees or plants have spread their roots into both. Before that, the imagination does not sufficiently join them.

There are other cases which somewhat resemble this matter of accession, but which, at the core, are considerably different and merit our attention. Of this kind is the joining of the properties of different persons in such a manner as not to allow for *separation*. The question is to whom the united mass must belong.

Where this joining is of such a nature as to allow for *division* but not *separation*, the decision is natural and easy. The whole mass must be supposed to be held in common between the owners of the several parts, and afterward must be divided according to the proportions of those parts. But

<margin>
SECTION III.
Of the rules which determine property.
</margin>
here I cannot help taking notice of a remarkable subtlety of the *Roman* law in distinguishing between *confusion* and *commixtion*. *Confusion* is a union of two bodies, such as different liquids, where the parts become entirely indistinguishable. *Commixtion* is the blending of two bodies, such as two bushels of corn, where the parts remain separate in an obvious and visible manner. As in the latter case the imagination does not discover so entire a union as in the former, but is able to trace and preserve a distinct idea of the property of each; this is the reason why the *civil law*, though it established an entire community in the case of *confusion*, and after that a proportional division, yet in the case of *commixtion*, supposes each of the proprietors to maintain a distinct right; however, necessity may at last force them to submit to the same division.

> *Quod si frumentum Titii frumento tuo mistum fuerit: siquidem ex voluntate vestra, commune est: quia singula corpora, id est, singula grana, quæ cujusque propria fuerunt, ex consensu vestro communicata sunt. Quod si casu id mistum fuerit, vel Titius id miscuerit sine tua voluntate, non videtur id commune esse: quia singula corpora in sua substantia durant. Sed nec magis istis casibus commune fit frumentum quam grex intelligitur esse communis, si pecora Titii tuis pecoribus mista fuerint. Sed si ab alterutro vestrûm totum id frumentum retineatur, in rem quidem actio pro modo frumenti cujusque competit. Arbitrio autem judicis, ut ipse æstimet quale cujusque frumentum fuerit.* <note>Institutes, Book II. Title 1. § 28.</note>

Where the properties of two persons are united in such a manner as to admit neither of division nor *separation*, as when one builds a house on another's ground, in that case, the whole must belong to one of the proprietors: And here I assert that it naturally is conceived to belong to the proprietor of the most considerable part. For however the compound object may have a relation to two different persons and carry our view at once to both of them, yet as the most considerable part principally engages our attention, and by the strict union draws the inferior along with it; for this reason, the whole bears a relation to the proprietor of that part and is regarded as his property. The only difficulty is what we shall be pleased to call the most considerable part, and most attractive to the imagination.

## PART II.

<margin>*Of justice and injustice.*</margin>
This quality depends on several different circumstances, which have little connection with each other. One part of a compound object may become more significant than another, either because it is more constant and durable; because it is of greater value; because it is more obvious and remarkable; because it is of greater extent; or because its existence is more separate and independent. It will be easy to conceive that, as these circumstances may be conjoined and opposed in all the different ways, and according to all the different degrees that can be imagined, there will result many cases where the reasons on both sides are so equally balanced that it is impossible for us to give any satisfactory decision. Here then is the proper business of municipal laws: to fix what the principles of human nature have left undetermined.

The <term>superficies</term> <note>In civil law, this refers to everything built upon or attached to the soil.</note> yields to the soil, says the civil law: the writing yields to the paper: the canvas yields to the picture. These decisions do not well agree together, and are a proof of the contrariety of those principles from which they are derived.

But of all the questions of this kind, the most curious is that which for so many ages divided the disciples of *Proculus* and *Sabinus*. Suppose a person should make a cup from the metal of another, or a ship from his wood, and suppose the proprietor of the metal or wood should demand his goods; the question is, whether he acquires a title to the cup or ship. *Sabinus* maintained the affirmative, and asserted that the substance or matter is the foundation of all the qualities; that it is incorruptible and immortal, and therefore superior to the form, which is casual and dependent. On the other hand, *Proculus* observed that the form is the most obvious and remarkable part, and that from it bodies are named as being of this or that particular species. To which he might have added that the matter or substance is in most bodies so fluctuating and uncertain that it is utterly impossible to trace it in all its changes. For my part, I know not from what principles such a controversy can be certainly determined. I shall therefore content myself with observing that the decision of *Trebonian* seems to me pretty ingenious: that the cup belongs to the proprietor of the metal because it can be brought back to its first form; but that the ship belongs to the author of its form for the opposite reason. But however ingenious this reason

<note>f</note> In examining the different titles to authority in government, we shall meet with many reasons to convince us that the right of succession depends, in a great measure, on the imagination. Meanwhile, I shall rest contented with observing one example, which belongs to the present subject. Suppose that a person dies without children, and that a dispute arises among his relations concerning his inheritance; it is evident that if his riches be derived partly from his father, partly from his mother, the most natural way of determining such a dispute is to divide his possessions and assign each part to the family from which it is derived. Now, as the person is supposed to have been once the full and entire proprietor of those goods; I ask, what is it that makes us find a certain equity and natural reason in this partition, except it be the imagination? His affection for these families does not depend upon his possessions; for which reason his consent can never be presumed precisely for such a partition. And as to the public interest, it seems not to be in the least concerned on one side or the other.

### SECT. IV.

->*Of the transference of property by consent.*<-

<margin>
SECT. IV.
<image-desc>flourish</image-desc>
</margin>HOWEVER useful, or even necessary, the stability of possession may be to human society, it is attended with very considerable inconveniences. The relation of fitness or suitableness ought never to enter into consideration in distributing the properties of mankind; but we must govern ourselves by rules which are more general in their application, and more free from doubt and uncertainty. Of this kind is *present possession* upon the first establishment of society; and afterwards *occupation*, *prescription*, *accession*, and *succession*. As these depend very much on chance, they must frequently prove contradictory both to men's wants and desires; and persons and possessions must often be very ill adjusted. This is a grand inconvenience, which calls for a remedy. To apply one directly, and allow every man to seize by violence what he judges to be fit for him, would destroy society; and therefore the rules of justice seek

seek some medium between a rigid stability and this changeable and uncertain adjustment. But there is no medium better than that obvious one: that possession and property should always be stable, except when the proprietor consents to bestow them on some other person. This rule can have no ill consequence in causing wars and dissensions, since the proprietor’s consent, who alone is concerned, is included in the alienation. And it may serve many good purposes in adjusting property to persons. Different parts of the earth produce different commodities; and not only so, but different men are both naturally fitted for different employments and attain greater perfection in any one when they confine themselves to it alone. All this requires a mutual exchange and commerce; for which reason the transfer of property by consent is founded on a law of nature, as well as its stability without such consent.

So far is determined by plain utility and interest. But perhaps it is for more trivial reasons that *delivery*, or a tangible transfer of the object, is commonly required by civil laws—and also by the laws of nature, according to most authors—as a necessary circumstance in the transfer of property.

<summary>Hume argues that property must be transferable by consent to enable trade and specialization, which are necessary because of the varied resources of the earth and the diverse talents of men. He suggests that while the principle of consent is rooted in utility, the legal requirement for the physical delivery of goods may arise from less significant reasons.</summary>

The property of an object, when taken for something real, without any reference to morality or the sentiments of the mind, is a quality perfectly imperceptible, and even inconceivable; nor can we form any distinct notion, either of its stability or its transfer. This imperfection of our ideas is felt less noticeably with regard to its stability, as it engages less of our attention and is easily passed over by the mind without any scrupulous examination. But as the transfer of property from one person to another is a more remarkable event, the defect of our ideas becomes more noticeable on that occasion and obliges us to turn ourselves on every side in search of some remedy. Now, as nothing enlivens any idea more than a present impression and a relation between that impression and the idea, it is natural for us to seek some false light from this quarter. In order to aid the imagination in conceiving the transference of property, we take the physical object and actually transfer its possession to the person on whom we would bestow the property. SECTION IV.
*Of the transference of property by consent.*

That this explanation of the matter is correct appears from the fact that men have invented a *symbolic* delivery to satisfy the imagination where a real delivery is impractical. Thus, the giving of the keys to a granary is understood to be the delivery of the grain contained in it; the giving of stone and earth represents the delivery of a manor. This is a kind of superstitious practice in civil laws and in the laws of nature, resembling *Roman Catholic* superstitions in religion. As the *Roman Catholics* represent the inconceivable mysteries of the *Christian* religion and make them more present to the mind by a candle, a religious garment, or a ritual gesture which is supposed to resemble them; so lawyers and moralists have fallen into similar inventions for the same reason and have endeavored by those means to satisfy themselves concerning the transference of property by consent.

PART II. these two propositions, which I proceed to prove, namely: *that a promise would not be intelligible before human conventions had established it; and that even if it were intelligible, it would not be accompanied by any moral obligation.*

I SAY, *first*, that a promise is not naturally intelligible, nor prior to human conventions; and that a man, unacquainted with society, could never enter into any engagements with another, even though they could perceive each other’s thoughts by intuition. If promises were natural and intelligible, there would have to be some act of the mind attending these words, *I promise*; and on this act of the mind the obligation would have to depend. Let us, therefore, examine all the faculties of the soul and see which of them is exerted in our promises.

THE act of the mind, expressed by a promise, is not a *resolution* to perform anything: For that alone never imposes any obligation. Nor is it a *desire* for such a performance: For we may bind ourselves without such a desire, or even with a declared and avowed aversion. Neither is it the *willing* of that action which we promise to perform: For a promise always regards some future time, and the will has an influence only

It follows, therefore, that since the act of the mind which enters into a promise and produces its obligation is neither the resolving, desiring, nor willing of any particular performance, it must necessarily be the *willing* of that *obligation* which arises from the promise. Nor is this only a conclusion of philosophy; it is entirely consistent with our common ways of thinking and of expressing ourselves when we say that we are bound by our own *consent*, and that the obligation arises from our mere will and pleasure. The only question, then, is whether there is not a manifest absurdity in supposing this act of the mind, and such an absurdity as no man could fall into whose ideas are not confounded by prejudice and the fallacious use of language.

ALL morality depends upon our sentiments; and when any action, or quality of the mind, pleases us *in a certain way*, we say it is virtuous; and when the neglect or non-performance of it displeases us *in a similar way*, we say that we are under an obligation to perform it. A change of the obligation supposes a change of the sentiment; and the creation of a new obligation supposes some new sentiment to arise. But it is certain we can naturally no more

It would be absurd, therefore, to will any new obligation—that is, any new sentiment of pain or pleasure; nor is it possible that men could naturally fall into such a gross absurdity. A promise, therefore, is *naturally* something altogether unintelligible, nor is there any act of the mind belonging to it <note>Were morality discoverable by reason, and not by sentiment, it would be still more evident that promises could make no alteration to it. Morality is supposed to consist in relations. Every new imposition of morality, therefore, must arise from some new relation of objects; and consequently, the will could not *immediately* produce any change in morals, but could have that effect only by producing a change in the objects themselves. But as the moral obligation of a promise is the pure effect of the will, without the least change in any part of the universe, it follows that promises have no *natural* obligation.</note>.

---

Should it be said that this act of the will, being in effect a new object, produces new relations and new duties, I would answer that this is a pure sophism, which may be detected by a very moderate share of accuracy and exactness. To will a new obligation is to will a new relation of objects; and therefore, if this new relation of objects were formed by the volition itself, we would in effect will the volition, which is plainly absurd and impossible. The will has here no object toward which it could tend, but must return upon itself *in infinitum*. The new obligation depends upon new relations,

<summary>Hume argues that since morality is based on human sentiments, which cannot be changed by an act of will, a promise cannot naturally create a moral obligation. He concludes that the idea of "willing" an obligation is a logical contradiction and that promises have no natural standing outside of social convention.</summary>

SECT. V.
{
Of the obligation of promises.
</margin>
But, *secondly*, if there were any act of the mind belonging to a promise, it could not *naturally* produce any obligation. This appears evidently from the preceding reasoning. A promise creates a new obligation. A new obligation supposes that new sentiments arise. The will *never* creates new sentiments. Therefore, no obligation could naturally arise from a promise, even supposing the mind could fall into the absurdity of willing that obligation.

The same truth may be proved even more evidently by the reasoning which proved justice in general to be an artificial virtue. No action can be required of us as our duty unless there is implanted in human nature some actuating passion or motive capable of producing the action. This motive cannot be the sense of duty. A sense of duty supposes an antecedent obligation; and where an action is not required by any natural passion, it cannot be required by any natural obligation, since it may be omitted without

---

The new relations depend upon a new volition. The new volition has for its object a new obligation, and consequently new relations, and consequently a new volition; which volition again has in view a new obligation, relation, and volition, without any end. It is impossible, therefore, that we could ever will a new obligation; and consequently it is impossible the will could ever accompany a promise, or produce a new moral obligation.

<meta>proving</meta>

<summary>Hume argues that promises do not naturally create moral obligations because the will cannot create new sentiments or motives, and a sense of duty requires a pre-existing natural passion. He further asserts that attempting to create an obligation through a simple act of will leads to an impossible infinite regress of volitions.</summary>

Now it is evident we have no motive leading us to the performance of promises, distinct from a sense of duty. If we thought that promises had no moral obligation, we would never feel any inclination to observe them. This is not the case with the natural virtues. Although there were no obligation to relieve the miserable, our humanity would lead us to it; and when we omit that duty, the immorality of the omission arises from its being a proof that we lack the natural sentiments of humanity. A father knows it to be his duty to take care of his children: But he also has a natural inclination to it. And if no human creature had that inclination, no one could lie under any such obligation. But as there is naturally no inclination to observe promises, distinct from a sense of their obligation; it follows that fidelity is no natural virtue, and that promises have no force prior to human conventions.

If anyone dissents from this, he must give a formal proof of these two propositions, namely: *that there is a peculiar act of the mind, annexed to promises; and that consequent to this act of the mind, there arises an*

In order to discover these necessities and interests, we must consider the same qualities of human nature which we have already found to give rise to the preceding laws of society. Because men are naturally selfish, or endowed only with a limited generosity, they are not easily induced to perform any action for the interest of strangers, except with a view to some reciprocal advantage which they had no hope of obtaining except by such a performance. Now, as it frequently happens that these mutual performances cannot be finished at the same instant, it is necessary that one party be content to remain in uncertainty and depend upon the gratitude of the other for a return of kindness. But there is so much corruption among men that, generally speaking, this becomes only a slender security; and as the benefactor is here supposed to bestow his favors with a view to self-interest, this both takes away from the obligation and sets an example of selfishness, which is the true

And we would perform just as few actions of that kind out of self-interest, because we cannot depend upon their gratitude. In this situation, the mutual exchange of good deeds is essentially lost among mankind, and everyone is reduced to their own skill and industry for their well-being and survival. One cannot transfer the property of a particular house, twenty leagues distant; because the consent cannot be accompanied by delivery, which is a required circumstance. Neither can one transfer the property of ten bushels of corn, or five barrels of wine, by the mere expression and consent; because these are only general terms, and have no direct relation to any particular heap of corn, or barrels of wine. Besides, the commerce of mankind is not confined to the barter of commodities, but may extend to services and actions, which we may exchange for our mutual interest and advantage. I have no kindness for you, and know you have as little for me. All this is the effect of the natural and inherent principles and passions of human nature; and as these passions and principles are unalterable, it may be thought that our conduct, which depends on them, must be so too, and that it would be in vain for either moralists or politicians to tamper with us, or attempt to change the usual course of our actions with a view to public interest. And indeed, if the success of their designs depended upon their success in correcting the selfishness and ingratitude of men, they would never make any progress, unless aided by omnipotence, which alone is able to remold the human mind and change its character in such fundamental articles. All they can pretend to do is to give a new direction to those natural passions, and teach us that we can better satisfy our appetites in an indirect and artificial manner than by their headlong and impetuous motion. Hence I learn to do a service to another without bearing him any real kindness, because I foresee that he will return my service in expectation of another of the same

But though this self-interested commerce of men begins to take place and to predominate in society, it does not entirely abolish the more generous and noble intercourse of friendship and good offices. I may still do services for such persons as I love and am more particularly acquainted with, without any prospect of advantage; and they may return the favor in the same manner, without any view other than that of recompensing my past services. In order, therefore, to distinguish those two different sorts of commerce—the interested and the disinterested—there is a *certain form of words* invented for the former, by which we bind ourselves to the performance of any action. This form of words constitutes what we call a *promise*, which is the sanction of the interested commerce of mankind. When a man says *he promises anything*, he in effect expresses a resolution of performing it; and along with that, by making use of this *form of words*,

A resolution is the natural act of the mind which promises express; but if there were nothing more than a resolution in the case, promises would only declare our former motives and would not create any new motive or obligation. They are the conventions of men that create a new motive, once experience has taught us that human affairs would be conducted much more for *mutual advantage* if there were certain *symbols* or *signs* instituted by which we might give each other security regarding our conduct in any particular incident. After these signs are instituted, whoever uses them is immediately bound by his interest to fulfill his engagements, and must never expect to be trusted again if he refuses to perform what he promised.

NOR is that knowledge, which is required to make mankind aware of this interest in the institution and *observance* of promises, to be considered superior to the capacity of human nature, however savage and uncultivated. It takes very little practice in the world to make us perceive all these consequences and advantages. The shortest experience of society reveals them to every person; and when each individual perceives

All of them, by concert, enter into a scheme of actions calculated for common benefit, and agree to be true to their word; nor is anything required to form this concert or convention, except that everyone has a sense of interest in the faithful fulfillment of engagements, and expresses that sense to other members of society. This immediately causes that interest to operate upon them; and interest is the *first* obligation for the performance of promises.

AFTERWARDS, a moral sentiment coincides with interest and becomes a new obligation for mankind. This sentiment of morality in the performance of promises arises from the same principles as that regarding the abstinence from the property of others. Public interest, education, and the artifices of politicians have the same effect in both cases. Here, therefore, we *feign* a new act of the mind, which we call the *willing* of an obligation; and on this we suppose the morality to depend. But we have already proved that there is no such act of the mind, and consequently that promises impose no natural obligation.

To confirm this, we may add some other reflections concerning that will which is supposed to enter into a promise and to cause its obligation. It is evident that the will alone is never supposed to cause the obligation, but must be expressed by words or signs in order to impose a tie upon any man. Once the expression is brought in as subservient to the will, it soon becomes the principal part of the promise; nor will a man be less bound by his word, even if he secretly gives a different direction to his intention and withholds himself both from a resolution and from willing an obligation. ## SECT. VI.

### *Some further reflections concerning justice and injustice.*

<margin>
SECT. VI.
</margin>
WE have now reviewed the three fundamental laws of nature: *the stability of possession, its transfer by consent, and the performance of promises.* It is on the strict observance of those three laws that the peace and security of human society entirely depend; nor is there any possibility of establishing good relations among men where these are neglected. Society is absolutely necessary for the well-being of men, and these laws are just as necessary for the support of society. Whatever restraint they may impose on the passions of men, they are the real offspring of those

### SECT. VI.
---

<margin>*Some further reflections concerning justice and injustice.*</margin>
Nothing is more vigilant and inventive than our passions; and nothing is more obvious than the agreement to follow these rules. Nature has, therefore, entrusted this matter entirely to human conduct, and has not placed in the mind any peculiar, original principles to direct us to a set of actions into which the other principles of our makeup and nature were sufficient to lead us. To convince us more fully of this truth, we may pause here for a moment and, from a review of the preceding arguments, may draw some new arguments to prove that those laws, however necessary, are entirely artificial and of human invention; and consequently that justice is an artificial, and not a natural, virtue.

I. THE first argument I shall use is derived from the common definition of justice. Justice is commonly defined to be *a constant and perpetual will of giving everyone their due.* In this definition, it is supposed that there are such things as right and property, independent of justice and prior to it; and that they would have existed even if men had never dreamed of practicing

practicing such a virtue. I have already observed, in a cursory manner, the fallacy of this opinion, and shall here continue to explain my sentiments on that subject a little more distinctly.

I SHALL begin by observing that this quality which we call *property* is like many of the imaginary qualities of the *peripatetic* philosophy, and vanishes upon a more accurate inspection of the subject when considered apart from our moral sentiments. It is evident that property does not consist in any of the sensible qualities of the object. For these may remain invariably the same while the property changes. Property, therefore, must consist in some relation of the object. But it is not in its relation with regard to other external and inanimate objects. For these may also remain invariably the same while the property changes. This quality, therefore, consists in the relations of objects to intelligent and rational beings. But it is not the external and physical relation which forms the essence of property. For that relation may be the same between inanimate objects, or with regard to brute creatures, though in those cases it forms no property. It is, therefore, in some internal relation that the property consists; that is,

<meta>catchword: in</meta>

Thus the external relation, which we call *occupation* or *first possession*, is not imagined to be the property of the object itself, but only to cause its property. Now it is evident that this external relation causes nothing in external objects, and has only an influence on the mind by giving us a sense of duty in abstaining from that object and in restoring it to the first possessor. These actions are properly what we call *justice*; and consequently, it is on that virtue that the nature of property depends, and not the virtue on the property.

If anyone, therefore, would assert that justice is a natural virtue, and injustice a natural vice, he must assert that—apart from the notions of *property*, *right*, and *obligation*—a certain conduct and series of actions, within certain external relations of objects, naturally has a moral beauty or deformity and causes an original pleasure or uneasiness. <margin>y</margin>*Secondly*, those rules by which properties, rights, and obligations are determined have no marks of a natural origin in them, but many marks of artifice and contrivance. They are too numerous to have proceeded from nature; they are changeable by human laws; and they all have a direct and evident tendency toward the public good and <margin>✓</margin>the support of civil society. This last circumstance is remarkable for two reasons. *First*, because even if the cause for establishing these laws had been a regard for the public good—as much as the public good is their natural tendency—they would still have been artificial, as they were purposely contrived and directed toward a certain end. *Secondly*, because if men had been endowed with such a strong regard for the public good, they would never have restrained themselves by these rules; so the laws of justice arise from natural principles in a manner that is even more oblique and artificial. It is self-love which is their real origin; and as the self-love of one person is naturally contrary to that of another, these various self-interested passions are obliged to adjust themselves in such a manner as to concur in a system

### II.

In the second place, we may observe that all kinds of vice and virtue blend into each other imperceptibly, and may approach by such subtle degrees that it will be very difficult, if not absolutely impossible, to determine where one ends and the other begins. From this observation, we may derive a new argument for the previous principle. For whatever the case may be regarding all kinds of vice and virtue, it is certain that rights, obligations, and property do not allow for such imperceptible gradations; rather, a person either has a full and perfect property or none at all, and is either entirely obliged to perform an action or is under no obligation whatsoever. However much civil laws may speak of a perfect *dominion* and an imperfect one, it is easy to observe that this arises from a fiction which has no foundation in reason and can never enter into our notions of natural justice and equity. A man who hires a horse, even if only for a day, has as full a right to

<meta>catchword: make</meta>

<summary>Hume argues that while virtues and vices blend together gradually, legal concepts like property and obligation are absolute and binary. He contends that the distinction between "perfect" and "imperfect" dominion in civil law is a legal fiction rather than a principle of natural justice.</summary>
<keywords>vice and virtue, property rights, legal obligation, dominion, natural justice, equity, civil law</keywords>

<meta>This page continues David Hume’s argument regarding the discrete nature of property rights and obligations, contrasting them with the gradual nature of other moral virtues and vices.</meta>

<margin>
S E C T.

---
Some farther reflections concerning justice and injustice.
</margin>
make use of it for that time, as he whom we call its proprietor has to make use of it any other day; and it is evident, that however the use may be bounded in time or degree, the right itself is not susceptible to any such gradation, but is absolute and entire, so far as it extends. Accordingly, we may observe that this right both arises and perishes in an instant; and that a man entirely acquires the property of any object by occupation, or the consent of the proprietor; and loses it by his own consent; without any of that imperceptible gradation, which is remarkable in other qualities and relations.

Since, therefore, this is the case with regard to property, and rights, and obligations, I ask, how does it stand with regard to justice and injustice? In whatever manner you answer this question, you run into inextricable difficulties. If you reply that justice and injustice admit of degrees, and run imperceptibly into each other, you expressly contradict the previous position, that obligation and property are not susceptible to such a gradation. These depend entirely upon justice and injustice, and follow them in all their variations. Where the justice is entire, the property is also entire; where the justice is imperfect, the property must also be imperfect.

<summary>Hume argues that property rights and obligations are absolute and arise or vanish instantly, rather than changing by degrees. He challenges the reader to explain how justice can be gradual if the property rights that depend on it are not.</summary>
<keywords>property rights, justice, injustice, obligation, proprietor, gradation of virtue, David Hume</keywords>

And *vice versa*, if property admits of no such variations, they must also be incompatible with justice. If you agree, therefore, to this last proposition, and assert that justice and injustice are not capable of degrees, you effectively assert that they are not naturally either vicious or virtuous; since vice and virtue, moral good and evil, and indeed all *natural* qualities, blend imperceptibly into each other and are, on many occasions, indistinguishable.

AND here it may be worthwhile to observe that, although abstract reasoning and the general maxims of philosophy and law establish this position—*that property, right, and obligation do not admit of degrees*—yet in our common and careless way of thinking, we find great difficulty in maintaining that opinion, and even *secretly* embrace the opposite principle. An object must be in the possession of either one person or another. An action must either be performed or not. The necessity of choosing one side in these dilemmas, and the frequent impossibility of finding any middle ground, obliges us—when we reflect on the matter—to acknowledge that all property and obligations are absolute. But on the other hand, when we consider the origin of property and obligation,

This is why, in cases of arbitration, where the consent of the parties leaves the referees as complete masters of the subject, they commonly find so much equity and justice on both sides that it induces them to strike a middle ground and divide the difference between the parties. Civil judges, who do not have this liberty but are obliged to give a decisive sentence for one side or the other, are often at a loss as to how to determine the outcome and are forced to proceed on the most frivolous reasons in the world. Half-rights and obligations, which seem so natural in everyday life, are complete absurdities in their courts; for this reason, they are often obliged to take half-arguments as whole ones in order to settle the affair one way or the other.

Since, therefore, men are so sincerely attached to their interest, and their interest is so much concerned in the observance of justice, and this interest is so certain and avowed; it may be asked, how any disorder can ever arise in society, and what principle there is in human nature so *powerful* as to overcome so strong a passion, or so *violent* as to obscure so clear a knowledge?

It has been observed, in treating of the passions, that men are powerfully governed by the imagination, and proportion their affections more to the light under which any object appears to them, than to its real and intrinsic value. What strikes upon them with a strong and lively idea commonly prevails above what lies in a more obscure light; and it must be a great superiority of value that is able to compensate this advantage. Now as everything that is contiguous to us, either in space or time, strikes upon us with such an idea, it has a proportional effect on the will and passions, and commonly operates with more force than any object that lies in a more distant and obscure light. This is the reason why men so often act in contradiction to their known interest; and in particular why they prefer any trivial advantage, that is present, to the maintenance of order in society, which so much depends on the observance of justice. ### Sect. VII.

---

<margin>
Of the
origin of
govern-
ment.
</margin>
The only difficulty, therefore, is to find this method by which people cure their natural weakness and place themselves under the necessity of observing the laws of justice and equity, despite their strong tendency to prefer what is immediate to what is distant. It is evident that such a remedy can never be effective without correcting this tendency; and as it is impossible to change or correct anything essential in our nature, the most we can do is change our circumstances and situation, making the observance of the laws of justice our most immediate interest and their violation our most distant concern. But since this is impractical for all of humanity, it can only take place for a few people, whose immediate interest we thus link to the execution of justice. These are the people we call civil magistrates, kings and their ministers, our governors and rulers. Because they are indifferent to the specific interests of the majority of the state, they have no interest—or only a very distant one—in any act of injustice. Being satisfied with their current status and their role in society, they have an immediate interest in every enforcement of justice, which is so necessary for maintaining 

<meta>catchword: society.</meta>

<summary>Hume argues that because human nature cannot be changed, society creates the "expedient" of government to make justice a matter of immediate interest. By appointing rulers and magistrates who are satisfied with their status, the enforcement of justice becomes their primary duty and personal benefit.</summary>
<keywords>origin of government, civil magistrates, justice, equity, human nature, social order, David Hume, political philosophy</keywords>

## PART II.

society. Here, then, is the origin of civil government and society. People are not able to radically cure, either in themselves or others, that narrowness of soul which makes them prefer the present to the remote. They cannot change their natures. All they can do is change their situation, and make the observance of justice the immediate interest of certain specific people, and its violation their more remote interest. These people, then, are not only induced to observe those rules in their own conduct, but also to constrain others to a similar regularity, and enforce the dictates of equity throughout the whole society. And if it is necessary, they may also interest others more immediately in the execution of justice, and create a number of officers, civil and military, to assist them in their government.

But this execution of justice, though the principal advantage, is not the only advantage of government. Just as violent passion hinders people from seeing clearly the interest they have in equitable behavior towards others, it also hinders them from seeing that equity itself, and gives them a remarkable partiality in their own favor. This inconvenience is corrected in the same manner as the one mentioned above. The same persons,

SECTION VII.
---
*Of the origin of government.*

By means of these two advantages, in the *execution* and *decision* of justice, people acquire a security against each other's weakness and passion, as well as against their own, and under the shelter of their governors, begin to enjoy at ease the pleasures of society and mutual assistance. But government extends its beneficial influence even further; and not content to protect people in those agreements they make for their mutual interest, it often obliges them to make such agreements, and forces them to seek their own advantage by cooperating toward some common end or purpose.

There is no quality in human nature which causes more fatal errors in our conduct than that which leads us to prefer whatever is present to the distant and remote, and makes us desire objects more according to their proximity than their intrinsic value. Two neighbors may agree to drain a meadow which they possess in common, because it is easy for them to know each other's mind; and each must perceive that the immediate consequence of failing in his part is the

But it is very difficult, and indeed impossible, that a thousand persons should agree in any such action; it being difficult for them to coordinate so complicated a plan, and still more difficult for them to execute it; while each seeks a pretext to free himself of the trouble and expense, and would lay the whole burden on others. Political society easily remedies both these inconveniences. Magistrates find an immediate interest in the interest of any considerable part of their subjects. They need consult nobody but themselves to form any plan for the promoting of that interest. And as the failure of any one part in the execution is connected, though not immediately, with the failure of the whole, they prevent that failure, because they find no interest in it, either immediate or remote. Thus bridges are built; harbors opened; ramparts raised; canals formed; fleets equipped; and armies disciplined; everywhere, by the care of government, which, though composed of men subject to all human infirmities, becomes, by one of the finest and most subtle inventions imaginable, a composition, <insert>that</insert> <margin>that</margin> which is, in some measure, exempted from all these infirmities.

Through government, society is able to build infrastructure and maintain public order, overcoming the natural limitations and "infirmities" of individual human behavior.</summary>

## S E C T. VIII.

### Of the source of allegiance.

<margin>
S E C T.
VIII.
</margin>
ALthough government is a very advantageous invention, and even absolutely necessary to mankind in some circumstances, it is not necessary in all circumstances; nor is it impossible for men to preserve society for some time without having recourse to such an invention. It is true that men are always very inclined to prefer present interests to those that are distant and remote; nor is it easy for them to resist the temptation of any advantage that they may immediately enjoy, out of fear of an evil that lies at a distance from them. But still, this weakness is less conspicuous where the possessions and the pleasures of life are few and of little value, as they always are in the infancy of society. An *Indian* is but little tempted to dispossess another of his hut, or to steal his bow, since he is already provided with the same advantages; and as for any superior fortune which may favor one person over another in hunting and fishing, it is only casual and temporary, and will have but small tendency

<meta>catchword: to</meta>

<summary>Hume argues that while government is beneficial and sometimes necessary, it is not essential in the earliest stages of society where few possessions exist to tempt people into conflict. In such simple conditions, the human tendency to prefer immediate gains over long-term stability is less damaging.</summary>
<keywords>allegiance, government, origins of society, self-interest, state of nature, property, human nature</keywords>
