And I am so far from thinking, as some philosophers do, that men are utterly incapable of living in a society without government, that I assert the first rudiments of government arise from quarrels—not among men of the same society, but among those of different societies. A smaller amount of wealth is enough to cause the latter effect than is required for the former. Men fear nothing from public war and violence except the resistance they encounter; because they share this resistance in common, it seems less terrible, and because it comes from strangers, it seems less harmful in its consequences than when they are exposed individually against someone whose trade is beneficial to them and without whose society it is impossible for them to survive. Now, for a society without government, foreign war necessarily produces civil war. Throw any significant goods among men, and they instantly start quarreling, while each strives to take possession of what pleases him without regard for the consequences. In a foreign war, the most significant of all goods—life and limbs—are at stake; and as everyone avoids dangerous positions, seizes the best weapons, and seeks excuses for even the slightest wounds, <margin>the rules of Society</margin> ~~the laws~~, which might be observed well enough while men were calm, can

<meta>catchword: now</meta>

<summary>Hume argues that government originates from foreign conflict rather than internal disputes, as the shared dangers of war inevitably lead to internal competition and the breakdown of peaceful social rules. We find this verified in the *American* tribes, where men live in concord and friendship among themselves without any established government. They never submit to any of their peers except in time of war, when their captain enjoys a shadow of authority. He loses this authority after their return from the field and the establishment of peace with neighboring tribes. This authority, however, instructs them in the advantages of government and teaches them to have recourse to it when—either by the spoils of war, by commerce, or by any lucky inventions—their riches and possessions have become so significant that they forget, in every emergency, the interest they have in the preservation of peace and justice. From this, we may give a plausible reason, among others, why all governments are at first monarchical, without any mixture or variety, and why republics arise only from the abuses of monarchy and despotic power. When men have once perceived the necessity of government to maintain peace and execute justice, they would naturally assemble together, would choose magistrates, determine their power, and *promise* them obedience. As a promise is supposed to be a bond or security already in use, and accompanied by a moral obligation, it is to be considered the original sanction of government and the source of the first obligation to obedience.

This reasoning appears so natural that it has become the foundation of our fashionable system of politics and is, in a way, the creed of a party among us who ~~pride~~ <insert>value</insert> themselves, with reason, on the soundness of their philosophy and their liberty of thought. *All men, they say, are born free and equal: Government and superiority can only be established by consent: The consent of men, in establishing government, imposes on them a new obligation, unknown to the laws of nature. ### SECTION VIII.
<margin>Of the source of allegiance.</margin> 

and those three fundamental laws of nature are as ancient as society: So that, taking advantage of the antiquity and obscure origin of these laws, they first deny that they are artificial and voluntary inventions of men, and then seek to graft onto them those other duties which are more plainly artificial. But once we are undeceived in this particular, and have found that *natural* as well as *civil justice* derives its origin from human conventions, we shall quickly perceive how fruitless it is to resolve one into the other, and to seek in the laws of nature a stronger foundation for our political duties than interest and human conventions—while these laws themselves are built on the very same foundation. Whichever way we turn this subject, we shall find that these two kinds of duty are on exactly the same footing and have the same source for both their *first invention* and their *moral obligation*. They are contrived to remedy similar inconveniences, and they acquire their moral sanction in the same manner: from their ability to remedy those inconveniences. These are two points which ~~we~~ we shall endeavor to prove as clearly as possible.

A Treatise of Human Nature.

<margin>
PART II.

Of justice and injustice.
</margin>

We have already shown that men *invented* the three fundamental laws of nature when they observed the necessity of society for their mutual survival, and found that it was impossible to maintain any social interaction together without some restraint on their natural appetites. The same self-love, therefore, which makes men so troublesome to each other, takes a new and more convenient direction, produces the rules of justice, and is the *first* motive for observing them. But when men have observed that although the rules of justice are sufficient to maintain any society, it is impossible for them, on their own, to observe those rules in large and sophisticated societies; they establish government as a new invention to attain their ends, and preserve the old advantages—or procure new ones—by a more strict execution of justice. To this extent, therefore, our *civil* duties are connected with our *natural* ones, in that the former are invented chiefly for the sake of the latter; and that the principal object of government is to compel men to observe the laws of nature. In this respect, however, that law of nature concerning the performance of promises is only included along with the rest; and its exact observance is to be considered as an effect of the insti-

<meta>catchword: tution</meta>

<summary>Hume explains that while justice was invented to restrain self-interest for the sake of social survival, government was later established to enforce these rules in large societies. Consequently, civil duties like political allegiance exist primarily to ensure the fulfillment of natural duties like justice and the keeping of promises.</summary>

Though the object of our civil duties is the enforcing of our natural duties, yet the <gloss>*</gloss> *first* motive for the invention, as well as the performance of both, is nothing but self-interest. And since there is a separate interest in obedience to government from that in the performance of promises, we must also allow for a separate obligation. To obey the civil magistrate is required to preserve order and concord in society. To perform promises is required to create mutual trust and confidence in the common affairs of life. The ends, as well as the means, are perfectly distinct; nor is the one subordinate to the other.

To make this more evident, let us consider that men will often bind themselves by promises to perform what it would have been their interest to perform independent of these promises; as when they would give others a fuller security by adding a new obligation of interest to that which they were previously under. The interest in the performance of promises, besides its moral obligation, is general, openly acknowledged, and of the highest consequence in life. Other interests

Now this is exactly the case with regard to our civil duties, or obedience to the magistrate; without which no government could subsist, nor any peace or order be maintained in large societies, where there are so many possessions on the one hand and so many wants, real or imaginary, on the other. A regard for property is not more necessary to natural society than obedience is to civil society or government; nor is the former society more necessary to the existence of mankind than the latter is to their well-being and happiness. In short, if the performance of promises is advantageous, so is obedience to government: If the former interest is general, so is the latter: If the one interest is obvious and avowed, so is the other. And as these two rules are founded on similar obligations of interest, each of them must have a unique authority, independent of the other.

But it is not only the *natural* obligations of interest which are distinct in promises and allegiance; but also the *moral* obligations of honor and conscience: Nor does the merit or demerit of the one depend in the least upon that of the other. And indeed, if we consider the close connection there is between the natural and moral obligations, we shall find this conclusion to be entirely unavoidable. Our interest is always engaged on the side of obedience to the magistracy; and there is nothing but a great present advantage that can lead us to rebellion,

*Of the source of allegiance.*

In the same way, as it is certain that there is a moral obligation to submit to government because everyone believes so, it must be equally certain that this obligation does not arise from a promise. No one whose judgment has not been misled by following a philosophical system too strictly has ever yet dreamed of attributing it to that origin. Neither magistrates nor subjects have formed this idea of our civil duties.

WE find that magistrates are so far from deriving their authority—and the obligation of obedience in their subjects—from the basis of a promise or original contract, that they conceal from their people (especially the common people) as much as possible that they have their origin from there. If this were the justification of government, our rulers would never accept it tacitly, which is the most that can be claimed. This is because what is given silently and unnoticed can never have as much influence on mankind as what is performed expressly and openly. A tacit promise is one where the will is expressed by other more indirect signs than speech; but there must certainly be a will involved in the case, and that can never escape the person's notice,

In consequence of this opinion, we frequently see people imagine that those who are currently deprived of all power and authority are their natural rulers—people whom no one, however foolish, would voluntarily choose. They do this merely because those people are in the lineage that ruled before and in that degree of succession which used to follow, though perhaps from such a distant period that hardly any man alive could ever have given a promise of obedience. Does a government, then, have no authority over people like these because they never consented to it and would consider the very attempt at such a free choice to be an act of arrogance and impiety? But as that is as *natural* and *common* a form of government as any, it must certainly create some obligation; and it is plain from experience that men who are subjected to it always think so.

</margin>
This is clear proof that we do not commonly consider our allegiance to be derived from our consent or promise. A further proof is that when our promise is expressly engaged for any reason, we always distinguish exactly between the two obligations, and believe the one to add more force to the other than a simple repetition of the same promise would. Where no promise is given, a man does not look on his word as broken in private matters because of rebellion; instead, he keeps those two duties of honor and allegiance perfectly distinct and separate. As the joining of them was thought by these philosophers to be a very <margin>*subtle*</margin> subtle invention, this is convincing proof that it is not a true one; since no man can either make a promise or be restrained by its sanction and obligation without knowing it himself.

...though the reasoning on which they endeavored to establish it was fallacious and sophistical. They would prove that our submission to government admits of exceptions, and that extreme tyranny in the rulers is sufficient to free the subjects from all ties of allegiance. Since men enter into society, they say, and submit themselves to government by their free and voluntary consent, they must have in view certain advantages which they propose to gain from it, and for which they are content to resign their natural liberty. There is, therefore, a mutual engagement on the part of the magistrate, namely protection and security; and it is only by the hope he offers of these advantages that he can ever persuade men to submit to him. But when, instead of protection and security, they encounter tyranny and oppression, they are freed from their promises (as happens in all conditional contracts) and return to that state of liberty which preceded the institution of government. Men would never be so foolish as to enter into engagements that would turn entirely to the advantage of others, without any prospect of improving their own condition. Whoever proposes to draw any profit from our submission must engage himself, either

<summary>Hume explains the social contract theory's argument that political allegiance is conditional; if a magistrate provides tyranny instead of protection, the subjects are released from their obligation to obey. This perspective holds that men only surrender natural liberty in exchange for the mutual benefits of security and order.</summary>

<page-num>160</page-num>
<header>*A Treatise of Human Nature.*</header>

<margin>PART II. Of justice and injustice.</margin>
expressly or tacitly, to make us reap some advantage from his authority; nor should he expect that, without performing his part, we will ever continue in obedience.

I REPEAT it: This conclusion is just, though the principles are erroneous; and I am confident that I can establish the same conclusion on more reasonable principles. I shall not take such a circuitous route in establishing our political duties as to assert that men perceive the advantages of government; that they institute government with a view to those advantages; that this institution requires a promise of obedience, which imposes a moral obligation to a certain degree, but being conditional, ceases to be binding whenever the other contracting party does not perform his part of the engagement. I perceive that a promise itself arises entirely from human conventions and is invented with a view to a certain interest. I seek, therefore, some such interest more immediately connected with government, which may be at once the original motive for its institution and the source of our obedience to it. This interest I find to consist in the security and protection which we enjoy in political society, and which we can never attain when perfectly free and independent.

As <note>The word "the" is crossed out with a horizontal pen line in the original.</note><del>the</del> interest, therefore, is the immediate sanction of government, the one can have no longer being than the other; and whenever the civil magistrate carries his oppression so far as to render his authority perfectly intolerable, we are no longer bound to submit to it. The cause ceases; the effect must cease also.

So far the conclusion is immediate and direct, concerning the *natural* obligation which we have to allegiance. As to the *moral* obligation, we may observe, that the maxim would here be false, that *when the cause ceases, the effect must cease also*. For there is a principle of human nature, which we have frequently taken notice of, that men are mightily addicted to *general rules*, and that we often carry our maxims beyond those reasons, which first induced us to establish them. Where cases are similar in many circumstances, we are apt to put them on the same footing, without considering, that they differ in the most material circumstances, and that the resemblance is more apparent than real. It may, therefore, be thought, that in the case of allegiance our moral obligation of duty will not cease, even though the natural obligation of interest, which is its cause, has ceased; and

Government, therefore, arises from the voluntary convention of men; and it is evident that the same convention which establishes government will also determine the persons who are to govern, and will remove all doubt and ambiguity in this particular. </margin>
The case here is the same as with that law of nature concerning the stability of possession. It is highly advantageous, and even absolutely necessary to society, that possession should be stable, and this leads us to establish such a rule. But we find that if we were to follow that same advantage in assigning specific possessions to specific people, we would fail in our goal and perpetuate the confusion that the rule is intended to prevent. We must, therefore, proceed by general rules and regulate ourselves by general interests when modifying the law of nature concerning the stability of possession. Nor should we fear that our attachment to this law will diminish because of the seeming triviality of those interests by which it is determined. The impulse of the mind is derived from a very strong interest, and those other smaller interests serve only to direct the motion without adding anything to it or taking anything away from it. It is the same case with government. Nothing is more advantageous to society than such an invention, and this interest is sufficient to make us embrace

THE *first* of those principles I shall take notice of, as a foundation of the right of magistracy, is that which gives authority to <insert>almost</insert> all the established governments of the world: I mean, *long possession* in any one form of government, or succession of princes. It is certain, that if we trace back to the first origin of every nation, we shall find that there is scarcely any race of kings, or form of a commonwealth, that is not primarily founded on usurpation and rebellion, and whose title is not at first worse than doubtful and uncertain. Time alone gives solidity to their right; and operating gradually on the minds of men, reconciles them to any authority, and makes it seem just and reasonable. Nothing causes any sentiment to have a greater influence upon us than custom, or turns our imagination more strongly to any object. WHEN there is no form of government established by *long possession*, the *present possession* is sufficient to supply its place, and may be regarded as the *second source* of all public authority. Right to authority is nothing but the constant possession of authority, maintained by the laws of society and the interests of mankind; and nothing can be more natural than to join this constant possession to the present one, according to the principles mentioned above. If the same principles did not take place with regard to the property of private persons, it was because these principles were counterbalanced by very strong considerations of interest; when we observed, that all restitution would by that means be prevented, and every violence be authorized and protected. And though the same motives may seem to have force, with regard to public authority, yet they are opposed by a contrary interest; which consists in the preservation of peace, and the avoiding of all changes, which, however they may be easily produced in private affairs, are unavoidably attended with blood-

### SECTION X.
---

<margin>*Of the objects of allegiance.*</margin>
**ANYONE** who, finding it impossible to account for the right of the current possessor by any received system of ethics, should resolve to absolutely deny that right and assert that it is not authorized by morality, would be justly thought to maintain a very extravagant paradox and to shock the common sense and judgment of mankind. No maxim is more consistent with both prudence and morals than to submit quietly to the government which we find established in the country where we happen to live, without inquiring too curiously into its origin and first establishment. Few governments will bear being examined so rigorously. How many kingdoms are there at present in the world, and how many more do we find in history, whose rulers have no better foundation for their authority than that of current possession? To limit ourselves to the *Roman* and *Greek* empires; is it not evident that the long succession of emperors—from the dissolution of *Roman* liberty to the final extinction of that empire by the *Turks*—could not even pretend to any other title to the empire? The election by the senate was a mere formality, which always

<summary>Hume argues that it is both prudent and moral to submit to an established government without overly scrutinizing its origins, as many great empires throughout history have been founded primarily on the fact of current possession.</summary>

followed the choice of the legions; and these were almost always divided in the different provinces, and nothing but the sword was able to end the dispute. It was by the sword, therefore, that every emperor acquired, as well as defended, his right; and we must either say that all the known world, for so many ages, had no government and owed no allegiance to anyone, or we must allow that the right of the stronger, in public affairs, is to be received as legitimate and authorized by morality, when not opposed by any other title.

The right of *conquest* may be considered a *third* source of the title of sovereigns. This right resembles very much that of *present possession*, but has rather a superior force, being supported by the notions of glory and honor which we attribute to *conquerors*, instead of the sentiments of hatred and detestation which accompany *usurpers*. Men naturally favor those they love; and therefore are more apt to attribute a right to successful violence between one sovereign and another, than to the successful rebellion of a subject against his sovereign*.

# Book III. Of Morals.

<margin>
SECT. X.
*Of the objects of allegiance.*
</margin>

WHEN neither long possession, nor current possession, nor conquest applies—as when the first sovereign who founded a monarchy dies—the right of *succession* naturally prevails in their place, and men are commonly induced to place the son of their late monarch on the throne and suppose him to inherit his father’s authority. The presumed consent of the father, the imitation of succession in private families, and the interest the state has in choosing the person who is most powerful and has the most numerous followers—all these reasons lead men to prefer the son of their late monarch to any other person^b.

THESE reasons have some weight; but I am persuaded that to anyone who considers the matter impartially, it will appear that some principles of the imagination <gloss>concur</gloss> <gloss>along</gloss> with those views of <gloss>justice and</gloss> interest. Royal authority seems to be connected with the young prince even during his father’s

---

be able to tip the balance where the titles are otherwise equal, and will even *sometimes* be sufficient to sanctify the weaker title. What degree of force they have is difficult to determine. I believe all moderate men will allow that they have great force in all disputes concerning the rights of princes.

^b To prevent mistakes, I must observe that this case of succession is not the same as that of hereditary monarchies, where custom has fixed the right of succession. These depend upon the principle of long possession explained above.

<summary>Hume explains that when a monarchy's founder dies, the right of succession typically passes to the son due to social stability, family analogies, and the father's presumed intent. He suggests that this preference is driven both by practical interest and the natural tendencies of the human imagination.</summary>

To confirm this, we may consider the following *phenomena*, which are quite curious in their nature. In elective monarchies, the right of succession has no place according to the laws and established customs; yet its influence is so natural that it is impossible to entirely exclude it from the imagination and make the subjects indifferent toward the son of their deceased monarch. Consequently, in some governments of this type, the choice usually falls on one member or another of the royal family; and in some governments, they are all excluded. These opposing *phenomena* arise from the same principle. Where the royal family is excluded, it is because of a refinement in politics that makes people aware of their tendency to choose a sovereign from that family. This gives them a jealousy regarding their liberty, fearing that their new monarch, aided by this tendency, might establish his family and destroy the freedom of future elections.

THE history of *Artaxerxes* and the younger *Cyrus* may provide us with some reflections to the same purpose. *Cyrus* claimed a right to the throne over his elder brother because he was born after his father's accession. I do not claim that this reason was valid. I would only infer from it that he would never have used such a pretext if it were not for the qualities of the imagination mentioned above, by which we are naturally inclined to unite, by a new relation, whatever objects we find already united. *Artaxerxes* had an advantage over his brother, as he was the eldest son and the first in succession; but *Cyrus* was more closely related to the royal authority, having been begotten after his father was invested with it.

tion; as it is natural to imagine. For instance; a government is established for many centuries on a certain system of laws, forms, and methods of succession. The legislative power, established by this long succession, changes all of a sudden the whole system of government, and introduces a new constitution in its place. I believe few of the subjects will think themselves bound to comply with this alteration, unless it has an evident tendency to the public good: But they will think themselves still at liberty to return to the ancient government. Hence the notion of *fundamental laws*; which are supposed to be unalterable by the will of the sovereign: And of this nature the *Salic* law is understood to be in *France*. How far these fundamental laws extend is not determined in any government; nor is it possible it ever should be. There is such an imperceptible gradation from the most material laws to the most trivial, and from the most ancient laws to the most modern, that it will be impossible to set bounds to the legislative power, and determine how far it may innovate in the principles of government. That is the work more of imagination and passion than of reason.

<summary>Hume explains that subjects often reject sudden constitutional changes unless they clearly benefit the public, leading to the concept of "fundamental laws" that are seen as unalterable by the sovereign. He notes that the exact boundaries of these laws are impossible to define rationally, as they depend more on imagination and historical sentiment.</summary>

# PART II.

<margin>Of justice and injustice.</margin>
WHOEVER considers the history of the various nations of the world; their revolutions, conquests, growth, and decline; the manner in which their particular governments are established, and the successive right transmitted from one person to another, will soon learn to treat very lightly all disputes concerning the rights of princes, and will be convinced, that a strict adherence to any general rules, and the rigid loyalty to particular persons and families, on which some people set so high a value, are virtues that stem less from reason, than from bigotry and superstition. In this particular, the study of history confirms the reasonings of true philosophy; which, showing us the original qualities of human nature, teaches us to regard the controversies in politics as incapable of any decision in most cases, and as entirely subordinate to the interests of peace and liberty. Where the public good does not evidently demand a change; it is certain, that the concurrence of all those titles, *original contract*, *long possession*, *present possession*, *succession*, and *positive laws*, forms the strongest title to sovereignty, and is justly regarded as sacred and inviolable. But when these titles are mingled and opposed in different degrees,

<summary>Hume argues that historical and philosophical study reveals that rigid loyalty to specific monarchs is often based on superstition rather than reason, as political disputes should be secondary to peace and liberty. He asserts that while a combination of traditional titles creates the strongest claim to sovereignty, these claims often conflict in practice.</summary>
<keywords>justice, injustice, sovereignty, original contract, human nature, philosophy, politics, loyalty, succession, public good</keywords>

Who shall tell me, for instance, whether *Germanicus* or *Drusus* ought to have succeeded *Tiberius*, had he died while they were both alive without naming either of them as his successor? Should the right of adoption be received as equivalent to that of blood in a nation where it had the same effect in private families, and had already, in two instances, taken place in public affairs? Should *Germanicus* be esteemed the eldest son because he was born before *Drusus*, or the younger because he was adopted after the birth of his brother? Should the right of the elder be respected in a nation where the eldest brother had no advantage in the succession of private families? Should the *Roman* empire at that time be esteemed hereditary because of two examples; or should it, even so early, be regarded as belonging to the stronger or the current possessor, as being founded on such a recent usurpation? Upon whatever principles we may claim to answer these and similar questions, I am afraid we shall never be able to satisfy an impartial inquirer who takes no side in political controversies,

But here an *English* reader will be likely to inquire concerning that famous *revolution*, which has had such a happy influence on our constitution, and has been attended with such mighty consequences. We have already remarked, that in the case of enormous tyranny and oppression, it is lawful to take up arms even against supreme power; and that as government is a mere human invention for mutual advantage and security, it no longer imposes any obligation, either natural or moral, once it ceases to have that tendency. But though this *general* principle is authorized by common sense, and the practice of all ages, it is certainly impossible for the laws, or even for philosophy, to establish any *particular* rules by which we may know when resistance is lawful, and decide all controversies which may arise on that subject. This may not only happen with regard to supreme power; but it is possible, even in some constitutions where the legislative authority is not lodged in one person, that there may be a magistrate so eminent and powerful as to oblige the laws to keep silence in this particular.

*First*, We may observe, that should the *lords* and *commons* in our constitution, without any reason from public interest, either depose the king in being, or after his death exclude the prince, who, by laws and settled custom, ought to succeed, no one would esteem their proceedings legal, or think themselves bound to comply with them. But should the king, by his unjust practices, or his attempts for a tyrannical and despotic power, justly forfeit his legal authority, it then not only becomes morally lawful and suitable to the nature of political society to dethrone him; but what is more, we are apt likewise to think, that the remaining members of the constitution acquire a right of excluding his next heir, and of choosing whom they please for his successor. This is founded on a very singular quality of our thought and imagination. The deposition of a king, in a government such as ours, is certainly an act beyond all common authority, and an illegal assumption of power for the public good, which, in the ordinary course of government, can belong to no member of the constitution. When the public good is so great and so evident as to justify the action, the commendable use of this license naturally causes us to attribute to the *parliament* a right of using further licenses; and once the ancient boundaries of the laws have been transgressed with approval, we are not inclined to be so strict in confining ourselves precisely within their limits. The mind naturally continues with any course of action which it has begun; nor do we commonly have any qualms concerning our duty after performing the first action of any kind.

Nor does the mind stop there; but returning back upon its footsteps, it transfers to their predecessors and ancestors that right which it naturally ascribes to their descendants, as they are related together and united in the imagination. The present king of *France* makes *Hugh Capet* a more lawful prince than *Cromwell*; just as the established liberty of the *Dutch* is a significant justification for their stubborn resistance to *Philip* II.

### *Of the laws of nations.*

<margin>

</margin>

<header>Book III. Of Morals.</header>
<meta>catchword: war.</meta>

<margin>

*Of the laws of nations.*
</margin>
other. Political writers tell us that in every kind of interaction, a body politic is to be considered as one person; and indeed this assertion is so far correct that different nations, as well as private persons, require mutual assistance, at the same time that their selfishness and ambition are perpetual sources of war and discord. But although nations in this particular resemble individuals, yet as they are very different in other respects, it is no wonder they regulate themselves by different maxims and give rise to a new set of rules, which we call *the laws of nations*. Under this heading, we may include the sacredness of the persons of ambassadors, the declaration of war, the abstaining from poisoned weapons, along with other duties of that kind, which are evidently designed for the interaction that is unique to different societies.

BUT although these rules are added to the laws of nature, the former do not entirely abolish the latter; and one may safely affirm that the three fundamental rules of justice—the stability of possession, its transfer by consent, and the performance of promises—are duties of princes as well as of subjects. The same interest produces the same effect in both cases. Where possession has no stability, there must be perpetual war.

<summary>Hume explains that while nations act like individual persons in their need for cooperation and their tendency toward conflict, their unique circumstances give rise to "the laws of nations," such as rules governing ambassadors and declarations of war. However, he emphasizes that these international rules do not replace the fundamental laws of nature regarding property and promises, which remain binding for rulers and subjects alike.</summary>

The advantages, therefore, of peace, commerce, and mutual succor make us extend to different kingdoms the same notions of justice that take place among individuals.

There is a maxim very common in the world, which few politicians are willing to admit, but which has been authorized by the practice of all ages: *that there is a system of morals intended for princes, much freer than that which ought to govern private persons.* It is evident this is not to be understood as a smaller extent of public duties and obligations; nor will anyone be so extreme as to assert that the most solemn treaties ought to have no force among princes. For as princes do actually form treaties among themselves, they must expect some advantage from their execution; and the prospect of such future advantage must engage them to perform their part and must establish that law of nature. The meaning, therefore, of this political maxim is that though the morality of princes has the same *extent*, yet it has not the same *force* as that of private persons,

<margin>
### SECTION IX.
Of the laws of nations.
</margin>

WHEN men have found by experience that it is impossible to subsist without society, and that it is impossible to maintain society while they give free course to their appetites, such an urgent interest quickly restrains their actions and imposes an obligation to observe those rules which we call the *laws of justice*. This obligation of interest does not rest here; but by the necessary course of the passions and sentiments, it gives rise to the moral obligation of duty, as we approve of such actions as tend toward the peace of society and disapprove of such as tend toward its disturbance. The same *natural* obligation of interest takes place among independent kingdoms and gives rise to the same *morality*; so that no one, even of the most corrupt morals, will approve of a prince who voluntarily and of his own accord breaks his word or violates any treaty. But here we may observe that although the intercourse between different states is advantageous, and even sometimes necessary, yet it is not as necessary or advantageous as

<meta>catchword: that</meta>

<summary>Hume explains that the laws of nations arise from the same practical necessity as the laws of justice among individuals: the need for social stability. However, he suggests that because international cooperation is less essential for survival than individual cooperation, the moral obligation for princes to follow these rules is proportionately weaker.</summary>

that among individuals, without which it is utterly impossible for human nature ever to subsist. Since, therefore, the *natural* obligation to justice among different states is not as strong as among individuals, the *moral* obligation which arises from it must partake of its weakness; and we must necessarily give a greater indulgence to a prince or minister who deceives another than to a private gentleman who breaks his word of honor.

SHOULD it be asked, *what proportion these two species of morality bear to each other?* I would answer that this is a question to which we can never give any precise answer; nor is it possible to reduce to numbers the proportion which we ought to fix between them. One may safely affirm that this proportion finds itself without any art or study of men, as we may observe on many other occasions. The practice of the world goes further in teaching us the degrees of our duty than the most subtle philosophy which was ever yet invented. And this may serve as a convincing proof that all men have an implicit notion of the foundation of those moral rules concerning natural and civil justice, and are sensible that they arise merely from human conventions and from the in-

## SECTION XII.

### *Of chastity and modesty.*

<margin>SECTION XII.</margin>
If any difficulty attends this system concerning the laws of nature and nations, it will be with regard to the universal approval or blame which follows their observance or transgression, and which some may not think is sufficiently explained by the general interests of society. To remove, as far as possible, all doubts of this kind, I shall here consider another set of duties, *namely* the *modesty* and *chastity* which belong to the fair sex: And I doubt not but these virtues will be found to be still more striking instances of the operation of those principles which I have emphasized.

There are some philosophers who attack female virtues with great vehemence and imagine they have gone very far in detecting popular errors when they can show that there is no foundation in nature for all that outward modesty which we require in the speech, dress, and behavior of the fair sex. I believe I may spare myself the trouble of dwelling on such an obvious subject and may proceed, without further preparation, to examine in what way such notions arise from education, from the voluntary conventions of men, and from the interest of society.

Whoever considers the length and weakness of human infancy, along with the concern that both sexes naturally have for their offspring, will easily perceive that there must be a union of male and female for the education of the young, and that this union must be of considerable duration. But in order to induce men to impose this restraint on themselves and cheerfully undergo all the hardships and expenses to which it subjects them, they must believe that the children are their own and that their natural instinct is not directed toward the wrong object when they give free rein to love and tenderness. And what possibility is there of instilling a reluctance to the approaches of a pleasure for which nature has inspired so strong a propensity—a propensity that it is absolutely necessary in the end to satisfy for the preservation of the species?

**COURAGE**, which is the point of honor among men, derives its merit in a great measure from artifice, as well as the chastity of women; though it also has some foundation in nature, as we shall see afterward.

As to the obligations that the male sex falls under with regard to chastity, we may observe that, according to the general notions of the world, they bear nearly the same proportion to the obligations of women as the obligations of the law of nations do to those of the law of nature. It is contrary to the interest of civil society for men to have an *entire* liberty of indulging their appetites in sexual enjoyment; but as this interest is weaker than in the case of the female sex, the moral obligation arising from it must be proportionally weaker. To prove this, we need only appeal to the practice and sentiments of all nations and ages.

<header>Book I. cap. III. Of Majesty.</header>
<warning>The text on this page is extremely faded and difficult to read, appearing as a ghost image or very light print. Transcription is aided by identifying the source text: Hugo Grotius, *The Rights of War and Peace*, 1738 edition.</warning>

...cannot take anything beyond the <unclear>express</unclear> words of the example, and <unclear>upon no account</unclear> the whole power or Majesty over the whole body; for if it had been meant to transfer and grant away such an authority,

Gronovius thinks it is the duty of those who believe such a power can consist in a grant—a grant other than what is extant in the words—to show how it can be consistent with the nature of things. For there is a foundation in nature on which laws may be built; but in the nature of things there are no laws except those founded in the will of the Lawgiver. And so it is the duty of those who assert that the whole and absolute power of Majesty resides in the people to show how that is consistent with the nature of things, and that the Law of Nature forbids a people from transferring their whole power and authority to another, so as to remain no longer in their own power. In some sense, in the case of a servant, the nature of things allows that he should be entirely under the master—that he should be unconditionally bound to his pleasure—and yet this is not against the Law of Nature; for if that were the case, there would be no slavery. This perhaps might be the case if we consider man as a creature endowed with reason, who can be under no obligation except such as he voluntarily takes on himself. The general Law of Nature, which allows that men should transfer their rights to another, does not determine what rights they shall transfer. It should be remembered, however, that the people are and continue to be the source and principle of all civil power.

#### *Of the origin of the natural virtues and vices.*

We come now to the examination of such virtues and vices as are entirely natural and have no dependence on the artifice and contrivance of men. The examination of these will conclude this system of morals.

The chief spring or actuating principle of the human mind is pleasure or pain; and when these sensations are removed, both from our thought and feeling, we are, in a great measure, incapable of passion or action, of desire or volition. These are diversified into volition, into desire and aversion, grief and joy, hope and fear, according to how the pleasure or pain changes its situation and becomes probable or improbable, certain or uncertain, or is considered as out of our power for the present moment. But when, along with this, the objects that cause pleasure or pain acquire a relation to ourselves or others, they still continue to excite desire and aversion, grief and joy; but they cause, at the same time, the indirect passions of pride or humility, love or hatred, which in this case have a double relation of impressions and ideas to the pain or pleasure.

WE have already observed that moral distinctions depend entirely on certain peculiar sentiments of pain and pleasure, and that whatever mental quality in ourselves or others gives us a satisfaction by its survey or reflection is, of course, virtuous; just as everything of this nature that gives uneasiness is vicious. Now, since every quality in ourselves or others that gives pleasure always causes pride or love, just as every quality that produces uneasiness excites humility or hatred, it follows that these two particulars are to be considered as equivalent with regard to our mental qualities: *virtue* and the power

If any *action* is either virtuous or vicious, it is only as a sign of some quality or character. It must depend upon durable principles of the mind, which extend over the person's whole conduct and enter into their personal character. Actions themselves, which do not proceed from any constant principle, have no influence on love or hatred, pride or humility; and consequently are never considered in morality.

This reflection is self-evident and deserves attention, as it is of the utmost importance to the present subject. We should never consider any single action in our inquiries concerning the origin of morals, but only the quality or character from which the action proceeded. These alone are *durable* enough to affect our sentiments concerning the person. Actions are, indeed, better indications of a character than words, or even wishes and sentiments; but it is only so far as they are such indications that they

To discover the true origin of morals, and of that love or hatred which arises from mental qualities, we must go quite deep into the matter and compare some principles which have been already examined and explained.

We may begin by reconsidering the nature and force of *sympathy*. The minds of all people are similar in their feelings and operations; nor can anyone be driven by any affection of which all others are not, to some degree, susceptible. As with strings tuned to the same pitch, the motion of one communicates itself to the rest; so all the affections readily pass from one person to another and create corresponding movements in every human being. When I see the *effects* of passion in the voice and gesture of any person, my mind immediately passes from these effects to their causes, and forms such a lively idea of the passion that it is presently converted into the passion itself. In the same way, when I perceive the *causes* of any emotion, my mind is led to the effects and is moved by a similar emotion. If I were present at any of the more terrible surgical operations,

<summary>Hume explains that sympathy is the mechanism by which emotions and passions are communicated between people, as the human mind is naturally susceptible to the feelings of others. He describes how observing the effects or causes of an emotion in someone else causes a similar emotion to arise within oneself.</summary>

preparation of the instruments, the laying of the bandages in order, the heating of the irons, with all the signs of anxiety and concern in the patient and assistants, would have a great effect upon my mind, and excite the strongest sentiments of pity and terror. No passion of another discovers itself immediately to the mind. We are only sensible of its causes or effects. From *these* we infer the passion: And consequently *these* give rise to our sympathy.

Our sense of beauty depends very much on this principle; and where any object has a tendency to produce pleasure in its possessor, it is always regarded as beautiful; as every object that has a tendency to produce pain is disagreeable and deformed. Thus the convenience of a house, the fertility of a field, the strength of a horse, the capacity, security, and swift sailing of a vessel, form the principal beauty of these several objects. Here the object, which is denominated beautiful, pleases only by its tendency to produce a certain effect. That effect is the pleasure or advantage of some other person. Now the pleasure of a stranger, for whom we have no friendship, pleases us only by sympathy. To this principle, therefore, is

The inventors of them had their own interest chiefly in view. But we extend our approval of them to the most distant countries and ages, and far beyond our own interest.

Now, as the means to an end can only be agreeable where the end itself is agreeable; and as the good of society, where our own interest or that of our friends is not concerned, pleases only by sympathy: It follows that sympathy is the source of the esteem which we pay to all the artificial virtues.

THUS it appears *that* sympathy is a very powerful principle in human nature, *that* it has a great influence on our taste for beauty, and *that* it produces our moral sentiment in all the artificial virtues. From this, we may presume that it also gives rise to many of the other virtues; and that qualities acquire our approval because of their tendency toward the good of mankind. </margin>
find that most of those qualities, which we *naturally approve of*, actually have that tendency and make a man a proper member of society; while the qualities which we *naturally disapprove of* have a contrary tendency and make any interaction with the person dangerous or unpleasant. Because we have found that such tendencies are strong enough to produce the strongest moral sentiment, we can never reasonably, in these cases, look for any other cause of approval or blame. It is an unbreakable maxim in philosophy that where any particular cause is sufficient for an effect, we should be satisfied with it and should not multiply causes without necessity. We have fortunately obtained evidence from experiments in the <term>artificial virtues</term>, where the tendency of qualities toward the good of society is the *sole* cause of our approval, without any suspicion that another principle is involved. From this, we learn the power of that principle. And where that principle may apply, and the quality approved of is truly beneficial to society, a true philosopher will never require any other principle to account for the strongest approval and esteem.

**THAT** many of the <term>natural virtues</term> have this tendency toward the good of society, no one <meta>catchword: can</meta>

<summary>Hume argues that since social utility is the sole cause for our approval of artificial virtues, the same principle should be used to explain our approval of natural virtues. He invokes the philosophical rule that one should not seek additional causes when a single sufficient cause has been identified.</summary>

can doubt. <margin>SECT. I. Of the origin of the natural virtues and vices.</margin> Meekness, beneficence, charity, generosity, clemency, moderation, and equity play the greatest part among the moral qualities, and are commonly called the *social virtues* to mark their tendency toward the good of society. This idea goes so far that some philosophers have represented all moral distinctions as the effect of artifice and education, where skillful politicians endeavored to restrain the turbulent passions of men and make them work for the public good through the notions of honor and shame. This system, however, is not consistent with experience. For, *first*, there are other virtues and vices besides those which have this tendency toward public advantage and loss. *Secondly*, if men did not have a natural sentiment of approval and blame, it could never be excited by politicians; nor would the words *laudable* and *praiseworthy*, *blameworthy* and *odious*, be any more intelligible than if they were a language perfectly unknown to us, as we have already observed. But though this system is erroneous, it may teach us that moral distinctions arise, in large part, from the tendency of qualities and characters to serve the interests of society, and that it is our concern for that interest which makes us approve or disapprove of them. Now we

THE only difference between the natural virtues and justice lies in this: that the good which results from the former arises from every single act and is the object of some natural passion; whereas a single act of justice, considered in itself, may often be contrary to the public good; and it is only the concurrence of mankind in a general scheme or system of action that is advantageous. When I relieve persons in distress, my natural humanity is my motive; and so far as my help extends, I have promoted the happiness of my fellow creatures to that extent. But if we examine all the questions that come before any tribunal of justice, we shall find that, considering each case separately, it would as often be an instance of humanity to decide contrary to the laws of justice as conformably to them. I MUST further add that there are several circumstances which render this hypothesis much more probable with regard to the natural than the artificial virtues. In any number of similar effects, if a cause can be discovered for one, we ought to extend that cause to all the other effects, which can be accounted for by it: But much more, if these other effects be attended with peculiar circumstances, which facilitate the operation of that cause.

BEFORE I proceed further, I must observe two remarkable circumstances in this affair, which may seem like objections to the present system. The first may be explained thus. When any quality, or character, has a tendency to the good of mankind, we are pleased with it, and approve of it; because it presents the lively idea of pleasure; which idea affects us by sympathy, and is itself a kind of pleasure. We sympathize more with people near to us than with people remote from us; with our acquaintances than with strangers; with our fellow citizens than with foreigners. But despite this variation in our sympathy, we give the same approval to the same moral qualities in *China* as in *England*. They appear equally virtuous and recommend themselves equally to the esteem of a judicious spectator. Sympathy varies without a variation in our esteem. Our esteem, therefore, does not proceed from sympathy.

To this I answer: The approval of moral qualities most certainly is not derived from reason or any comparison of ideas, but proceeds entirely from a moral taste, and from certain sentiments of pleasure or disgust that arise upon the contemplation and view of particular qualities or characters. Now it is evident that those sentiments, wherever they are derived from, must vary according to the distance or proximity of the objects; nor can I feel the same lively pleasure from the virtues of a person who lived in *Greece* two thousand years ago that I feel from the virtues of a familiar friend and acquaintance. But to consider the <margin>Case</margin><insert>case</insert> ~~matter~~ correctly, it has no force at all; and it is the easiest matter in the world to account for it. Our situation, with regard both to persons and things, is in continual fluctuation; and a man who is at a distance from us may, in a little time, become a familiar acquaintance. Besides, every particular man has a unique position with regard to others; and it would be impossible for us to ever converse together on any reasonable terms if each of us were to consider characters and persons only as they appear from his own unique point of view. In order, therefore, to prevent those continual *contradictions*, and arrive at a more *stable* judgment of things, we fix on some *steady* and *general* points of view; and always, in our thoughts, place ourselves in them, whatever may be our present situation. In like manner, external beauty is determined merely by pleasure; and it is evident that a beautiful countenance cannot give as much pleasure when seen at the distance of twenty paces as when it is brought nearer to us. IN general, all sentiments of blame or praise are variable, according to our situation of nearness or remoteness with regard to the person blamed or praised, and according to the present disposition of our mind. But these variations we do not regard in our general decisions, but still apply the terms expressive of our liking or dislike in the same manner as if we remained in one point of view. Experience soon teaches us this method of correcting our sentiments, or at least of correcting our language, where the sentiments are more stubborn and unalterable. Our servant, if diligent and faithful, may excite stronger sentiments of love and kindness than *Marcus Brutus*, as represented in history; but we do not say upon that account that the former character is more laudable than the latter. We know that were we to approach equally near to that renowned patriot, he would command a much higher degree of affection and admiration. It is therefore from the influence of characters and qualities upon those who have an intercourse with any person that we blame or praise him. We do not consider whether the persons affected by the qualities are our acquaintances or strangers, fellow countrymen or foreigners. Indeed, we overlook our own interest in those general judgments and do not blame a man for opposing us in any of our pretensions when his own interest is particularly concerned. We make allowance for a certain degree of selfishness in men, because we know it to be inseparable from human nature and inherent in our frame and constitution. By this reflection, we correct those sentiments of blame which so naturally arise upon any opposition.

The meaning of this is that we know from reflection that the former action would excite as strong sentiments of disapprobation as the latter, were it placed in the same position.

I now proceed to the *second* remarkable circumstance which I proposed to notice. Where a person possesses a character that, in its natural tendency, is beneficial to society, we esteem him virtuous and are delighted with the view of his character, even though particular accidents prevent its operation and incapacitate him from being serviceable to his friends and country. Sympathy interests us in the good of mankind; and if sympathy were the source of our esteem for virtue, that sentiment of approval could only take place where the virtue actually attained its end and was beneficial to mankind. Where it fails to achieve its end, it is only an imperfect means; and therefore can never acquire any merit from that end. The goodness of an end can bestow merit only on such means as are complete and actually produce the end.

To this we may reply that where any object, in all its parts, is suited to attain any agreeable end, it naturally gives us pleasure and is esteemed beautiful, even though some external circumstances are lacking to render it altogether effective. It is sufficient if everything is complete in the object itself. A house that is designed with great judgment for all the conveniences of life pleases us on that account, even though perhaps we are aware that no one will ever live in it. A fertile soil and a happy climate delight us by reflecting on the happiness they would afford the inhabitants, even though at present the country is a desert and uninhabited. When a character is, in every respect, suited to be beneficial to society, the imagination passes easily from the cause to the effect, without considering that there are still some circumstances lacking to render the cause a complete one. *General rules* create a species of probability, which sometimes influences the judgment, and always the imagination.

It is true, when the cause is complete, and a good disposition is attended with good fortune, which renders it really beneficial to society, it gives a stronger pleasure to the spectator, and is attended with a more lively sympathy. We are more affected by it; and yet we do not say that it is more virtuous, or that we esteem it more. We know, that an alteration of fortune may render the benevolent disposition entirely powerless; and therefore we separate, as much as possible, the fortune from the disposition. The passions do not always follow our corrections; but these corrections serve sufficiently to regulate our abstract notions, and are alone regarded when we pronounce in general concerning the degrees of vice and virtue.

It is observed by critics that all words or sentences which are difficult to pronounce are disagreeable to the ear. There is no difference whether a man hears them pronounced or reads them silently to himself. When I run over a book with my eye, I imagine I hear it all; and also, by the force of imagination, enter into the uneasiness which the delivery of it would give the speaker. The uneasiness is not real; but as such a composition of words has a natural tendency to produce it, this is sufficient to affect the mind with a painful sentiment and render the discourse harsh and disagreeable. <margin>style</margin> It is a similar case where any real quality is, by accidental circumstances, rendered powerless, and is deprived of its natural influence on society.

Sentiments must touch the heart to make them control our passions: But they need not extend beyond the imagination to make them influence our taste. When a building seems clumsy and tottering to the eye, it is ugly and disagreeable; though we are fully assured of the solidity of the workmanship. It is a kind of fear which causes this sentiment of disapprobation; but the passion is not the same as that which we feel when obliged to stand under a wall that we really think tottering and insecure. It is very fortunate, in our philosophical research, when we find the same phenomena diversified by a variety of circumstances; and by discovering what is common among them, we can better assure ourselves of the truth of any hypothesis we may use to explain <insert>that phenomenon</insert>. If nothing were esteemed a virtue except what was beneficial to society, I am persuaded that the previous explanation of the moral sense should still be received, and that upon sufficient evidence. But this evidence must grow stronger when we find other kinds of virtue which will not admit of any explanation except from that hypothesis. Here is a man who is not remarkably defective in his social qualities; but what principally recommends him is his skill in business, by which he has freed himself from the greatest difficulties and conducted the most delicate affairs with a remarkable skill and prudence. I find an esteem for him immediately arises in me: his company is a satisfaction to me; and before I have any further acquaintance with him, I would rather do him a service

than another whose character is equal in every other respect but is deficient in that particular one. In this case, the qualities that please me are all considered useful to the person, and as having a tendency to promote his interest and satisfaction. They are only regarded as means to an end and please me in proportion to their fitness for that end. Therefore, the end must be agreeable to me. But what makes the end agreeable? The person is a stranger; I am in no way interested in him, nor do I lie under any obligation to him. His happiness does not concern me any further than the happiness of every human—and indeed, of every sentient creature. That is, it affects me only through sympathy. Based on that principle, whenever I discover his happiness and well-being, whether in its causes or its effects, I enter into it so deeply that it gives me a noticeable emotion. The appearance of qualities that have a tendency to promote his happiness has an agreeable effect upon my imagination and commands my love and esteem.

THIS theory may serve to explain why the same qualities always produce both pride and love, or humility and hatred; and why the same man is always considered virtuous or vicious, accomplished or despicable to others,

A person in whom we discover any passion or habit that is originally only inconvenient to himself always becomes disagreeable to us merely on that account; just as, on the other hand, someone whose character is only dangerous and disagreeable to others can never be satisfied with himself as long as he is aware of that disadvantage. Nor is this observable only regarding characters and manners, but it can be remarked even in the most minute circumstances. A violent cough in another person gives us uneasiness, although in itself it does not affect us in the least. A man will be mortified if you tell him he has bad breath, even though it is clearly no annoyance to himself. Our imagination easily changes its perspective; by either surveying ourselves as we appear to others or considering others as they feel themselves, it <insert>makes us</insert> enter into sentiments that in no way belong to us, and in which nothing but sympathy is able to interest us. And we sometimes carry this sympathy so far as even to be displeased with a quality beneficial to us, merely because it displeases others and <insert>renders</insert> us disagreeable in their eyes, even though we may never have any interest in making ourselves agreeable to them.

<margin>
PART III.
___
Of the other virtues and vices.
</margin>

There have been many systems of morality advanced by philosophers in all ages; but if they are strictly examined, they may be reduced to two, which alone merit our attention. Moral good and evil are certainly distinguished by our *sentiments*, not by *reason*: But these sentiments may arise either from the mere appearance of characters and passions, or from reflections on their tendency toward the happiness of mankind and of particular individuals. My opinion is that both these causes are intermixed in our judgments of morals, in the same way they are in our decisions concerning most kinds of external beauty. Though I am also of the opinion that reflections on the tendencies of actions have by far the greatest influence and determine all the major lines of our duty. There are, however, instances in cases of less importance where this immediate taste or sentiment produces our approval. Wit and a certain easy and relaxed behavior are qualities *immediately agreeable* to others and command their love and esteem. Some of these qualities produce satisfaction in others through particular *original* principles of human nature that cannot be explained. Others may be resolved into principles, which

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

<summary>Hume argues that moral judgments arise from two sources: the immediate agreeable appearance of a character and reflections on how actions contribute to human happiness. While immediate sentiment accounts for qualities like wit, he maintains that the utility of actions is the primary influence on our sense of moral duty.</summary>
<keywords>morality, sentiments, reason, human nature, external beauty, wit, virtue, vice, utility, David Hume</keywords>

### SECT. I.

---

<margin>*Of the origin of the natural virtues and vices.*</margin>
Just as some qualities gain their merit from being *immediately agreeable* to others, without any tendency toward the public interest; so some are designated virtuous because they are *immediately agreeable* to the person himself who possesses them. Each of the passions and operations of the mind has a particular feeling, which must be either agreeable or disagreeable. The first is virtuous, the second vicious. This particular feeling constitutes the very nature of the passion; and therefore does not need to be explained.

But however directly the distinction of vice and virtue may seem to flow from the immediate pleasure or uneasiness which particular qualities cause to ourselves or others, it is easy to observe that it also has a considerable dependence on the principle of *sympathy* so often emphasized. We approve of a person who possesses qualities *immediately agreeable* to those with whom he has any interaction, even though we ourselves perhaps never reaped any pleasure from them. We also approve of one who possesses qualities that are *immediately agreeable* to himself, even though they are of no service to

<meta>catchword: any</meta>

<summary>Hume explains that some virtues are defined by being immediately agreeable to the possessor or to others, independent of public utility. He notes that our approval of these qualities often relies on sympathy, allowing us to value traits that benefit others even when they do not benefit us personally.</summary>

THUS, to take a general review of the present hypothesis: Every quality of the mind is termed virtuous if it gives pleasure by the mere survey of it, just as every quality that produces pain is called vicious. This pleasure and this pain may arise from four different sources. For we derive pleasure from the view of a character that is naturally suited to be useful to others or to the person themselves, or which is agreeable to others or to the person themselves. One may, perhaps, be surprised that amidst all these interests and pleasures, we should forget our own, which affects us so closely on every other occasion. But we shall easily satisfy ourselves on this point when we consider that, because every individual person's pleasure and interest are different, it is impossible for men to ever agree in their sentiments and judgments unless they choose some common point of view from which they might survey their object, and which might cause it to appear the same to all of them. Now, in judging characters, the only interest and pleasure that appears the same to every spectator is that of the person themselves whose character is examined, or that

And though such interests and pleasures touch us more faintly than our own, yet being more constant and universal, they counterbalance the latter even in practice, and are alone admitted in speculation as the standard of virtue and morality. They alone produce that particular feeling or sentiment on which moral distinctions depend.

As to the good or ill desert of virtue or vice, it is an evident consequence of the sentiments of pleasure or uneasiness. These sentiments produce love or hatred; and love or hatred, by the original constitution of human passion, is attended with benevolence or anger; that is, with a desire of making happy the person we love, and miserable the person we hate. We have treated of this more fully on another occasion.

We shall begin by examining the passions of *pride* and *humility*, and shall consider the vice or virtue that lies in their excesses or just proportion. An excessive pride or over-weening conceit of ourselves is always esteemed vicious and is universally hated; just as modesty, or a just sense of our weakness, is esteemed virtuous and procures the goodwill of everyone. Of the four sources of moral distinctions, this is to be ascribed to the *third*: namely, the immediate agreeableness and disagreeableness of a quality to others, without any reflections on the tendency of that quality.

In order to prove this, we must have recourse to two principles which are very conspicuous in human nature. The *first* of these is the *sympathy* and communication of sentiments and passions mentioned above. So close and intimate is the correspondence of human souls that no sooner does any person approach me than he diffuses all his opinions onto me and draws my judgment along in a greater or lesser degree. And though, on many occasions, my sympathy with him does not go so far as entirely to change my sentiments and way of thinking, yet it is seldom so weak as not to disturb the easy course of

This principle of sympathy is of so powerful and insinuating a nature that it enters into most of our sentiments and passions, and often takes place under the appearance of its contrary. For it is remarkable that when a person opposes me in anything which I am strongly bent upon, and rouses up my passion by contradiction, I have always a degree of sympathy with him, nor does my commotion proceed from any other origin. We may here observe an evident conflict or encounter of opposite principles and passions. On the one side there is that passion or sentiment which is natural to me; and it is observable that the stronger this passion is, the greater is the commotion. There must also be some passion or sentiment on the other side; and this passion can proceed from nothing but sympathy. The

While they remain concealed in the minds of others, they can never have any influence upon us: And even when they are known, if they went no further than the imagination or conception, that faculty is so accustomed to objects of every different kind that a mere idea, though contrary to our sentiments and inclinations, would never alone be able to affect us.

THE second principle I shall take notice of is that of *comparison*, or the variation of our judgments concerning objects according to the proportion they bear to those with which we compare them. We judge more of objects by comparison than by their intrinsic worth and value, and regard everything as mean when set in opposition to what is superior of the same kind. But no comparison is more obvious than that with ourselves; and hence it is that on all occasions it takes place and mixes with most of our passions. This kind of comparison is directly contrary to sympathy in its operation,

The direct survey of another's pleasure naturally gives us pleasure; and therefore produces pain when compared with our own. His pain, considered in itself, is painful; but it augments the idea of our own happiness and gives us pleasure.*

Since, then, those principles of sympathy and a comparison with ourselves are directly contrary, it may be worthwhile to consider what general rules can be formed—besides the particular temper of the person—for the prevalence of the one or the other. Suppose I am now in safety on land and would willingly reap some pleasure from this consideration: I must think on the miserable condition of those who are at sea in a storm, and I must endeavor to make this idea as strong and lively as possible in order to make me more sensible of my own happiness. But whatever pains I may take, the comparison will never have an equal efficacy,

as if I were really on <sup>b</sup> the shore, and saw a ship at a distance, tossed by a tempest, and in danger every moment of perishing on a rock or sandbank. But suppose this idea to become still more lively. Suppose the ship to be driven so near me, that I can perceive distinctly the horror painted on the countenances of the seamen and passengers, hear their lamentable cries, see the dearest friends give their last farewell, or embrace with a resolution to perish in each other's arms: No man has so savage a heart as to derive any pleasure from such a spectacle, or withstand the impulses of the tenderest compassion and sympathy. It is evident, therefore, there is a middle ground in this case; and that if the idea is too faint, it has no influence through comparison; and on the other hand, if it is too strong, it operates on us entirely through sympathy, which is the opposite of comparison. Sympathy, being the conversion of an idea into an impression, demands a greater force and vivacity in the idea than is required for comparison.

## PART
## III.
---
<margin>Of the other virtues and vices.</margin>
The firm persuasion he has of his own merit takes hold of the imagination and diminishes us in our own eyes, in the same manner as if he were really possessed of all the good qualities which he so liberally attributes to himself. Our idea is here precisely in that middle ground which is required to make it operate on us by comparison. If it were accompanied by belief, and if the person appeared to have the same merit which he assumes for himself, it would have the opposite effect and would operate on us through sympathy. The influence of that principle would then be superior to that of comparison, contrary to what happens when the person's merit seems below his pretensions.

The necessary consequence of these principles is that pride, or an overweening conceit of ourselves, must be a vice; since it causes uneasiness in all men and presents them every moment with a disagreeable comparison. It is a trite observation in philosophy, and even in common life and conversation, that it is our own pride which makes us so displeased with the pride of other people; and that vanity becomes insupportable to us merely because we are vain. The cheerful naturally associate themselves with the cheerful, and the amorous with the

As we are, all of us, proud to some degree, pride is universally blamed and condemned by all mankind, as it has a natural tendency to cause uneasiness in others by means of comparison. And this effect must follow all the more naturally because those who have an ill-grounded conceit of themselves are forever making those comparisons, nor do they have any other method of supporting their vanity. A man of sense and merit is pleased with himself, independent of all external considerations; but a fool must always find some person who is more foolish in order to keep himself in good humor with his own abilities and understanding.

But though an overweening conceit of our own merit is vicious and disagreeable, nothing can be more laudable than to have a value for ourselves where we really possess qualities that are valuable. directly to show that passion. We all have a wonderful partiality for ourselves, and were we always to give vent to our sentiments in this particular, we would mutually cause the greatest indignation in each other, not only by the immediate presence of so disagreeable a subject of comparison, but also by the opposition of our judgments. In the same way, therefore, as we establish the *laws of nature* in order to secure property in society and prevent the opposition of self-interest; we establish the *rules of good breeding* in order to prevent the opposition of men’s pride and render conversation agreeable and inoffensive. Nothing is more disagreeable than a man’s overweening conceit of himself: almost everyone has a strong propensity to this vice; no one can easily distinguish in *himself* between the vice and virtue, or be certain that his esteem of his own merit is well-founded. For these reasons, all direct expressions of this passion are condemned; nor do we make any exception to this rule in favor of men of sense and merit. They are not allowed to do themselves justice openly in words any more than other people; and even if they show a reserve and secret doubt in doing themselves justice in
