That impertinent and almost universal tendency of men to overvalue themselves has given us such a *prejudice* against *self-applause* that we are apt to condemn it, by a *general rule*, wherever we meet with it; and it is with some difficulty that we grant a privilege to men of sense, even in their most *secret* thoughts. At least, it must be owned that some disguise in this particular is absolutely necessary; and that if we harbor pride in our hearts, we must maintain a fair outward appearance and show the appearance of modesty and mutual deference in all our conduct and behavior. We must, on every occasion, be ready to prefer others to ourselves; to treat them with a kind of deference, even though they are our equals; to seem always the lowest and least in the company, where we are not very much distinguished above them: And if we observe these rules in our conduct, men will have more indulgence for our secret sentiments when we reveal them in an *oblique manner*.

### SECTION II.

---

<margin>Of greatness of mind.</margin>
On the contrary, we may observe that a genuine and hearty pride, or self-esteem, if well concealed and well founded, is essential to the character of a man of honor, and that there is no quality of the mind which is more indispensably requisite to procure the esteem and approbation of mankind. There are certain deferences and mutual submissions which custom requires of the different ranks of men towards each other; and whoever exceeds in this particular, if through interest, is accused of meanness; if through ignorance, of simplicity. It is necessary, therefore, to know our rank and station in the world, whether it be fixed by our birth, fortune, employments, talents, or reputation. It is necessary to feel the sentiment and passion of pride in conformity to it, and to regulate our actions accordingly. And should it be said that prudence may suffice to regulate our actions in this particular, without any real pride, I would observe that here the object of prudence is to conform our actions to the general usage and custom; and that it is impossible those tacit airs of superiority should ever have been established

<summary>Hume argues that a well-founded and properly concealed sense of pride is essential to a person of honor and necessary for gaining social respect. He asserts that individuals must understand their social rank—whether determined by birth, fortune, or talent—and feel a pride that corresponds to that station to avoid being seen as either mean or ignorant.</summary>

If we pass from common life and conversation to history, this reasoning acquires new force, when we observe that all those great actions and sentiments, which have become the admiration of mankind, are founded on nothing but pride and self-esteem. "Go," says *Alexander* the Great to his soldiers, when they refused to follow him to the *Indies*, "*go tell your countrymen, that you left Alexander completing the conquest of the world.*" This passage was always particularly admired by the prince of *Conde*, as we learn from St. *Evremond*. “*Alexander*,” said that prince, “abandoned by his soldiers, among barbarians, not yet fully subdued, felt in himself such a dignity and right of empire, that he could not believe it possible anyone could refuse to obey him. Whether in *Europe* or in *Asia*, among *Greeks* or *Persians*, all was indifferent to him: Wherever he found men, he fancied he had found subjects.”

In general we may observe, that whatever we call *heroic virtue*, and admire under the character of greatness and elevation of mind, is either nothing but a steady and well-established pride and self-esteem, or

Courage, intrepidity, ambition, love of glory, magnanimity, and all the other brilliant virtues of that kind, plainly have a strong mixture of self-esteem in them, and derive a great part of their merit from that origin. Accordingly, we find that many religious critics denounce those virtues as purely pagan and natural, and represent to us the excellence of the *Christian* religion, which places humility in the rank of virtues and corrects the judgment of the world, and even of philosophers, who so generally admire all the efforts of pride and ambition. Whether this virtue of humility has been rightly understood, I shall not pretend to determine. I am content with the admission that the world naturally esteems a well-regulated pride, which secretly motivates our conduct without breaking out into such unbecoming expressions of vanity as may offend the vanity of others.

The merit of pride or self-esteem is derived from two circumstances, namely its utility and its agreeableness to ourselves; by which it prepares us for business, and, at the same time, gives us an immediate satisfaction. When it goes beyond its just bounds, it loses the first advantage, and even becomes harmful;

No one who properly considers this matter will hesitate to admit that any instance of ill-breeding, or any expression of pride and haughtiness, is displeasing to us merely because it shocks our own pride and leads us through sympathy into a comparison which causes the disagreeable passion of humility. Now, as insolence of this kind is blamed even in a person who has always been civil to us personally—indeed, even in someone whose name we only know from history—it follows that our disapproval proceeds from a sympathy with others, and from the reflection that such a character is highly displeasing and hateful to everyone who talks to or has any interaction with the person who possesses it. We sympathize with those people in their discomfort; and as their discomfort proceeds in part from a sympathy with the person who insults them, we may observe here a double rebound of sympathy, which is a principle very similar to what we have observed on another occasion <sup>a</sup>.

## S E C T. III.

**H**AVING thus explained the origin of that praise and approval which accompanies everything we call *great* in human affections, we now proceed to give an account of their *goodness* and show from where its merit is derived.

When experience has once given us a sufficient knowledge of human affairs and has taught us the proportion they bear to human passion, we perceive that the generosity of men is very limited and that it seldom extends beyond their friends and family, or, at most, beyond their native country. Being thus acquainted with the nature of man, we do not expect any impossibilities from him; instead, we confine our view to that narrow circle in which any person moves in order to form a judgment of his moral character. When the natural tendency of his passions leads him to be helpful and useful within his sphere, we approve of his character and love his person through a sympathy

We are quickly obliged to forget our own interest in our judgments of this kind because of the perpetual contradictions we meet with in society and conversation from persons who are not placed in the same situation and do not have the same interest as ourselves. The only point of view in which our sentiments concur with those of others is when we consider the tendency of any passion to the advantage or harm of those who have any immediate connection or intercourse with the person possessed of it. And though this advantage or harm is often very remote from ourselves, yet sometimes it is very near us and interests us strongly by sympathy. This concern we readily extend to other cases that are similar; and when these are very remote, our sympathy is proportionally weaker, and our praise or blame fainter and more doubtful. The case here is the same as in our judgments concerning external bodies. All objects seem to diminish by their distance: But though the appearance of objects to our senses is the original standard by which we judge them, yet we do not say that they actually diminish by distance; but correcting the ap-

<margin>*Of goodness and benevolence.*</margin>
### S E C T. III.
---

In like manner, though sympathy is much fainter than our concern for ourselves, and a sympathy with persons remote from us is much fainter than that with persons near and close by; yet we neglect all these differences in our calm judgments concerning the characters of men. Besides, since we ourselves often change our situation in this particular, we every day meet with persons who are in a different situation from ourselves, and who could never converse with us on any reasonable terms were we to remain constantly in that situation and point of view which is peculiar to us. The intercourse of sentiments, therefore, in society and conversation, makes us form some general, unalterable standard by which we may approve or disapprove of characters and manners. And though the *heart* does not always take part in those general notions, or regulate its love and hatred by them, yet they are sufficient for discourse and serve all our purposes in company, in the pulpit, in the theater, and in the schools.

From these principles, we may easily account for that merit which is commonly ascribed to *generosity, humanity, compassion,*

While our emotions may not always align with this abstract standard, it provides a necessary common language for evaluating character in public and private life.</summary>

An inclination toward the tender passions makes a person agreeable and useful in all parts of life; it gives a proper direction to all of one's other qualities, which otherwise might become harmful to society. Courage and ambition, when not regulated by benevolence, are fit only to create a tyrant and public robber. It is the same case with judgment and ability, and all qualities of that kind. They are indifferent in themselves to the interests of society, and have a tendency toward the good or ill of mankind, depending on how they are directed by these other passions.

As love is *immediately agreeable* to the person who is moved by it, and hatred *immediately disagreeable*, this may also be a significant reason why we praise all the passions that partake of the former and blame all those that have any considerable share of the latter. It is certain that we are infinitely touched by a tender sentiment as much as by a great one. All this seems to me to be proof that our approval, in those cases, has an origin different from the prospect of utility and advantage, either to ourselves or to others. To this we may add that people naturally, without reflection, approve of the character that is most like their own. A person of a mild disposition and tender affections, when forming a concept of the most perfect virtue, includes more benevolence and humanity in it than a person of courage and enterprise does, who naturally views a certain elevation of mind as the most accomplished character. This must evidently proceed from an *immediate sympathy* that people have with characters similar to their own. They enter into such sentiments with more warmth and feel the pleasure arising from them more deeply.

It is remarkable that nothing touches a humane person more than an instance of extraordinary delicacy in love or friendship, where a person is attentive to the smallest concerns of their friend and is willing to sacrifice their own most significant interests for them. Such delicacies have little influence on society because they make us value the smallest trifles; but they are all the more engaging the more minute the concern is, and

Anger and hatred are passions inherent in our very frame and constitution. The lack of them, on some occasions, may even be a proof of weakness and imbecility. And where they appear only in a low degree, we not only excuse them because they are natural; but even bestow our applause on them, because they are lesser than what appears in the majority of mankind.

WHERE these angry passions rise up to cruelty, they form the most detested of all vices. All the pity and concern which we have for the miserable sufferers of this vice turns against the person guilty of it, and produces a stronger hatred than we feel on any other occasion.

EVEN when the vice of inhumanity does not rise to this extreme degree, our sentiments concerning it are very much influenced by reflections on the harm that results from it. And we may observe in general, that if we can find any quality in a person which renders him inconvenient to those who live and converse with him, we always allow it to be a fault or blemish, without any further examination. On the other hand, when we enumerate the good qualities of any person, we always mention those parts of his character,

We consider him in all his relations in society; and love or hate him according to how he affects those who have any immediate intercourse with him. And it is a most certain rule that if there is no relation of life in which I would not wish to stand to a particular person, his character must be allowed to be perfect to that extent. If he is as little lacking to himself as to others, his character is entirely perfect. This is the ultimate test of merit and virtue.

No distinction is more common in all systems of ethics than that between *<term>natural abilities</term>* and *<term>moral virtues</term>*; where the former are placed on the same footing as bodily endowments, and are supposed to have no merit or moral worth attached to them. and that although these qualities are not altogether of the same kind, they still agree in the most important circumstances. They are both equally mental qualities, and both equally produce pleasure; consequently, they have an equal tendency to gain the love and esteem of mankind. There are few who are not as protective of their reputation regarding their intelligence and knowledge as they are regarding their honor and courage—and much more so than regarding their temperance and sobriety. Men are even afraid of being seen as good-natured, lest *that* should be mistaken for a lack of understanding. They often boast of more debaucheries than they have actually engaged in, simply to give themselves an air of passion and spirit. In short, the impression a man makes in the world, the reception he meets with in company, and the esteem shown to him by his acquaintances—all these advantages depend almost as much on his good sense and judgment as on any other part of his character. Even if a man has the best intentions in the world and is completely free from all injustice and violence, he will never be highly regarded without at least a moderate amount of talent and understanding. Since natural abilities, though perhaps inferior, are nonetheless on the

same footing, both in their causes and effects, as those qualities which we call moral virtues, why should we make any distinction between them?

Though we refuse to grant natural abilities the title of virtues, we must admit that they earn the love and esteem of mankind; that they give a new luster to the other virtues; and that a person possessed of them is much more entitled to our goodwill and services than one who is entirely void of them. It may, indeed, be argued that the sentiment of approval which those qualities produce, besides being *inferior*, is also somewhat *different* from that which accompanies the other virtues. But this, in my opinion, is not a sufficient reason for excluding them from the list of virtues. Each of the virtues—even benevolence, justice, gratitude, and integrity—excites a different sentiment or feeling in the observer. The characters of *Caesar* and *Cato*, as drawn by *Sallust*, are both virtuous in the strictest sense of the word, but in different ways; nor are the feelings entirely the same which arise from them. SECTION IV.
*Of natural abilities.*
</margin>

In the same way, the approval that accompanies natural abilities may feel somewhat different from that which arises from the other virtues, without making them an entirely different species. And indeed, we may observe that natural abilities, no more than the other virtues, do not all produce the same kind of approval. Good sense and genius produce esteem; wit and humor excite love<sup>a</sup>.

Those who represent the distinction between natural abilities and moral virtues as very significant may say that the former are entirely involuntary and therefore have no merit attached to them, as they have no dependence on liberty and free will. But to this I answer, *first*, that many of those qualities which all moralists—especially the ancients—include under the title of moral virtues are equally involuntary and necessary as the qualities of the judgment and imagina-

---

<note><sup>a</sup> Love and esteem are at their core the same passions and arise from similar causes. The qualities that produce both are agreeable and provide pleasure. But where this pleasure is severe and serious, or where its object is great and makes a strong impression, or where it produces any degree of humility and awe: in all these cases, the passion that arises from the pleasure is more properly called esteem than love. Benevolence accompanies both, but is connected with love to a much greater degree.</note>

### PART III.

<margin>

*Of the other virtues and vices.*

Constancy, fortitude, magnanimity, and, in short, all the qualities that form the *great man* are of this nature. I might say the same, to some extent, of other qualities; it is almost impossible for the mind to change its character in any significant way, or to cure itself of a passionate or irritable temper when these are natural to it. The greater the degree of these blameworthy qualities, the more vicious they become, and yet they are less voluntary.

*Secondly*, I would like anyone to give me a reason why virtue and vice may not be involuntary, just as beauty and deformity are. These moral distinctions arise from the natural distinctions of pain and pleasure; and when we experience those feelings from the general consideration of any quality or character, we call it vicious or virtuous. Now, I believe no one will assert that a quality can never produce pleasure or pain for the person observing it, unless it is perfectly voluntary in the person who possesses it. *Thirdly*, regarding free will, we have shown that it has no place in relation to actions, any more than it does to the qualities of men. It is not a logical conclusion that what is voluntary is therefore free. Our actions are more voluntary than our judgments; but we

<summary>Hume argues that many virtues, such as fortitude and magnanimity, are involuntary traits of character, yet they still evoke moral approval or disapproval. He contends that moral distinctions are based on the pleasure or pain a quality produces in an observer, regardless of whether the trait was chosen through free will.</summary>

<margin>
S E C T.
IV.
<image-desc>wavy horizontal line ornament</image-desc>
*Of natural abilities.*
</margin>
BUT although this distinction between voluntary and involuntary is not sufficient to justify the distinction between natural abilities and moral virtues, yet the former distinction will provide us with a plausible reason why moralists have invented the latter. Men have observed that, although natural abilities and moral qualities are in the main on the same footing, there is, however, this difference between them: the former are almost unchangeable by any art or industry; while the latter—or at least the actions that proceed from them—may be changed by the motives of rewards and punishments, praise and blame. Hence legislators, theologians, and moralists have principally applied themselves to regulating these voluntary actions, and have endeavored to produce additional motives for being virtuous in that particular regard. They knew that punishing a man for folly, or exhorting him to be prudent and sagacious, would have very little effect; though the same punishments and exhortations, with regard to justice and injustice, might have a considerable influence. But as men, in common life and conversation, do not keep those ends in view, but naturally praise or blame

<meta>catchword: whatever</meta>

<summary>Hume argues that while natural abilities and moral virtues are fundamentally similar, the distinction between them was created because moral actions can be influenced by rewards and punishments. He notes that while social authorities use this distinction to regulate behavior, ordinary people naturally praise or blame traits regardless of whether they are voluntary.</summary>

Indeed, we find that all moralists, whose judgment is not perverted by a strict adherence to a system, share the same way of thinking; and that the ancient moralists in particular had no hesitation in placing prudence at the head of the cardinal virtues. There is a sentiment of esteem and approbation which may be excited, in some degree, by any faculty of the mind in its perfect state and condition; and to account for this sentiment is the business of *Philosophers*. It belongs to *Grammarians* to examine what qualities are entitled to the name of *virtue*; nor will they find, upon trial, that this is as easy a task as they might be inclined to imagine at first sight.

The principal reason why natural abilities are esteemed is because of their tendency to be useful to the person who possesses them. It is impossible to execute any design with success where it is not conducted with prudence and discretion; nor will the goodness of our intentions alone be enough to provide us a happy outcome to our enterprises.

<margin>
Sect.
IV.
---
*Of natural abilities.*
</margin>
Men are superior to beasts principally by the superiority of their reason; and it is the various degrees of this same faculty which create such an infinite difference between one man and another. All the advantages of art are due to human reason; and where fortune is not very capricious, the most significant part of these advantages must fall to the share of the prudent and sagacious.

**When** it is asked whether a quick or a slow apprehension is most valuable; whether one person, who penetrates into a subject at first glance but can achieve nothing through study, is better than a contrary character who must work out everything through diligent application; whether a clear head or a fertile invention is superior; whether a profound genius or a reliable judgment is better—in short, what character or specific understanding is more excellent than another? It is evident we can answer none of these questions without considering which of those qualities best enables a man for the world and carries him farthest in any of his undertakings.

**There** are many other qualities of the mind whose merit is derived from the same origin. *Industry*, *perseverance*, *patience*, *activity*, *vigilance*, *application*, *constancy*, along with other virtues of that kind, which it will be

It is the same case with *temperance, frugality, economy, and resolution*: just as, on the other hand, *prodigality, luxury, irresolution, and uncertainty* are vicious, merely because they bring ruin upon us and incapacitate us for business and action.

As wisdom and good sense are valued because they are *useful* to the person who possesses them, so *wit* and *eloquence* are valued because they are *immediately agreeable* to others. On the other hand, *good humor* is loved and esteemed because it is *immediately agreeable* to the person himself. It is evident that the conversation of a man of wit is very satisfying; just as a cheerful, good-humored companion diffuses joy over the whole company through a sympathy with his gaiety. These qualities, therefore, being agreeable, naturally produce love and esteem, and meet all the criteria of virtue.

It is difficult to tell, on many occasions, what it is that renders one man’s conversation so agreeable and entertaining and another's so insipid and distasteful. As conversation is a transcript of the mind just as books are, the same qualities which make the one valuable,

<margin>*Of natural abilities.*</margin>
### SECTION IV.

---

In the meantime, it may be affirmed in general that all the merit a man may derive from his conversation (which, no doubt, may be very considerable) arises from nothing but the pleasure it conveys to those who are present.

In this view, *cleanliness* is also to be regarded as a virtue, since it naturally renders us agreeable to others and is a very considerable source of love and affection. No one will deny that a negligence in this particular is a fault; and as faults are nothing but smaller vices, and this fault can have no other origin than the uneasy sensation which it excites in others, we may in this instance—seemingly so trivial—clearly discover the origin of the moral distinction between vice and virtue in other instances.

Besides all those qualities which render a person lovely or valuable, there is also a certain *je ne sais quoi* of the agreeable and handsome that contributes to the same effect. In this case, as well as in that of wit and eloquence, we must have recourse to a certain sense which acts without reflection and does not regard the tendencies of qualities and characters. Some moralists account for all

THE *decorum* or *indecorum* of a quality, with regard to the age, character, or station, contributes also to its praise or blame. This decorum depends, in a great measure, upon experience. It is usual to see men lose their levity as they advance in years. Such a degree of gravity, therefore, and such years, are connected together in our thoughts. When we observe them separated in any person’s character, this imposes a kind of violence on our imagination and is disagreeable.

<margin>SECTION IV. *Of natural abilities.*</margin>
It is so far from being a virtue to have a good memory, that men generally affect to complain of a bad one; and endeavoring to persuade the world that what they say is entirely of their own invention, they sacrifice it to the praise of genius and judgment. Yet to consider the matter abstractedly, it would be difficult to give a reason why the faculty of recalling past ideas with truth and clearness should not have as much merit in it as the faculty of placing our present ideas in such an order as to form true propositions and opinions. The reason of the difference certainly must be that the memory is exerted without any sensation of pleasure or pain; and in all its middling degrees serves almost equally well in business and affairs. But the least variations in the judgment are sensibly felt in their consequences; while at the same time that faculty is never exerted in any eminent degree without an extraordinary delight and satisfaction. The sympathy with this utility and pleasure bestows a merit on the understanding; and the absence of it makes us

## PART III.

---

<margin>*Of the other virtues and vices.*</margin>
Before I leave this subject of *natural* abilities, I must observe that, perhaps, one source of the esteem and affection which attends them is derived from the *importance* and *weight* which they bestow on the person possessed of them. He becomes of greater consequence in life. His resolutions and actions affect a greater number of his fellow-creatures. Both his friendship and enmity are of moment. And it is easy to observe that whoever is elevated in this manner above the rest of mankind must excite in us the sentiments of esteem and approbation. Whatever is important engages our attention, fixes our thought, and is contemplated with satisfaction. The histories of kingdoms are more interesting than domestic stories; the histories of great empires more than those of small cities and principalities; and the histories of wars and revolutions more than those of peace and order. We sympathize with the persons who suffer in all the various sentiments that belong to their fortunes. The mind is occupied by the multitude of the objects and by the strong passions that display themselves. And this occupation or agitation of the mind is

### SECT. V.
<image-desc>decorative calligraphic flourish</image-desc>

Another source of the pleasure we receive from considering bodily advantages is their utility to the person himself who possesses them. It is certain that a considerable part of the beauty of men, as well as of other animals, consists in such a conformation of limbs as we find by experience to be accompanied by strength and agility, and to enable the creature for any action or exercise. Broad shoulders, a flat belly, firm joints, tapered legs—all these are beautiful in our species because they are signs of force and vigor; since these are advantages we naturally sympathize with, they convey to the beholder a share of that satisfaction they produce in the possessor.

How far the advantages of *fortune* produce esteem and approval from the same principles, we may satisfy ourselves by reflecting on our previous reasoning on that subject. We have observed that our approval of those who possess the advantages of fortune may be ascribed to three different causes. *First*, to that immediate pleasure which a rich man gives us by the sight of the beautiful clothes, equipage, gardens, or houses which he possesses. *Secondly*, to the advantage which we hope to gain from him through his generosity and liberality. *Thirdly*, to the pleasure and advantage,

Whether we attribute our esteem of the rich and great to one or all of these causes, we may clearly see the traces of those principles which give rise to the sense of vice and virtue. I believe most people, at first sight, will be inclined to attribute our esteem of the rich to self-interest and the prospect of advantage. But as it is certain that our esteem or deference extends beyond any prospect of advantage to ourselves, it is evident that this sentiment must proceed from a sympathy with those who are dependent on the person we esteem and respect, and who have an immediate connection with him. We consider him as a person capable of contributing to the happiness or enjoyment of his fellow creatures, whose sentiments, with regard to him, we naturally embrace. And this consideration will serve to justify my hypothesis in preferring the *third* principle to the other two, and attributing our esteem of the rich to a sympathy with the pleasure and advantage which they themselves receive from their possessions. For as even the other two principles cannot operate to a due extent, or account for all the phenomena, without having recourse to sympathy

In like manner, whenever we survey the actions and characters of men, without any particular interest in them, the pleasure or pain which arises from the survey (with some minute differences) is, in the main, of the same kind, though perhaps there is a great diversity in the causes from which it is derived. On the other hand, a convenient house and a virtuous character do not cause the same feeling of approval, even though the source of our approval is the same and flows from sympathy and an idea of their utility. There is something very inexplicable in this variation of our feelings; but it is what we experience with regard to all our passions and sentiments.

We find that it has enough power to give us the strongest sentiments of approval when it operates alone, without the cooperation of any other principle—as in the cases of justice, allegiance, chastity, and good manners. We may observe that all the circumstances required for its operation are found in most of the virtues, which for the most part have a tendency toward the good of society or toward the good of the person possessed of them. If we compare all these circumstances, we shall not doubt that sympathy is the chief source of moral distinctions, especially when we reflect that no objection can be raised against this hypothesis in one case that will not extend to all cases. Justice is certainly approved of for no other reason than because it has a tendency toward the public good; and the public good is indifferent to us, except insofar as sympathy interests us in it. ### SECT. VI.

<margin>Conclusion of this book.</margin>
Most people will readily allow that the useful qualities of the mind are virtuous because of their utility. This way of thinking is so natural and occurs on so many occasions that few will have any hesitation in admitting it. Now, once this is admitted, the power of sympathy must necessarily be acknowledged. Virtue is considered as a means to an end. Means to an end are only valued as much as the end itself is valued. But the happiness of strangers affects us by sympathy alone. To that principle, therefore, we must attribute the sentiment of approval, which arises from the survey of all those virtues that are useful to society or to the person who possesses them. These form the most significant part of morality.

If it were appropriate in such a subject to bribe the reader’s agreement, or to employ anything but solid argument, we would here be abundantly supplied with topics to engage the emotions. All lovers of virtue (and we are all such in theory, however much we may degenerate in practice) must certainly be pleased to see

<vocab>morality, sympathy, virtue, utility, sentiment of approval</vocab>

<summary>Hume concludes that the utility of mental qualities is a primary reason we consider them virtuous, and this utility only gains moral value through the mechanism of sympathy. He argues that the majority of moral virtues are those that are useful either to society or to the individuals who possess them.</summary>
<keywords>morality, sympathy, utility, virtue, moral approval, social benefit, David Hume</keywords>

It requires very little knowledge of human affairs to perceive that a sense of morals is a principle inherent in the soul, and one of the most powerful that enters into its composition. But this sense must certainly acquire new force when, reflecting on itself, it approves of those principles from which it is derived, and finds nothing but what is great and good in its rise and origin. Those who resolve the sense of morals into original instincts of the human mind may defend the cause of virtue with sufficient authority; but they lack the advantage possessed by those who account for that sense through an extensive sympathy with mankind. According to <margin>this latter</margin> system, not only must virtue be approved of, but also the sense of virtue—and not only that sense, but also the principles from which it is derived. So that nothing is presented on any side but what is laudable and good.

This observation may be extended to justice and other virtues of that kind. Though justice is artificial, the sense of its morality is natural. It is the combination of men in a system of conduct which renders

Most human inventions are subject to change. They depend on humor and caprice. They are fashionable for a time and then sink into oblivion. It might, perhaps, be feared that if justice were allowed to be a human invention, it must be placed on the same footing. But the cases are widely different. The interest on which justice is founded is the greatest imaginable and extends to all times and places. It cannot possibly be served by any other invention. It is obvious and reveals itself at the very first formation of society. All these causes render the rules of justice steadfast and immutable—at least as immutable as human nature. And if they were founded on original instincts, could they have any greater stability?

An anatomist, however, is admirably suited to give advice to a painter; and it is even impossible to excel in the latter art without the assistance of the former. We must have an exact knowledge of the parts, their position and connection, before we can design with any elegance or correctness. And thus the most abstract speculations concerning human nature, however cold and unentertaining, become useful to *practical morality*; and may render this latter science more correct in its principles, and more persuasive in its exhortations.

# APPENDIX

There is nothing I would more willingly lay hold of than an opportunity to confess my errors; and I would consider such a return to truth and reason to be more honorable than the most unerring judgment. A man who is free from mistakes can claim no praise, except for the soundness of his understanding; but a man who corrects his mistakes shows at once the soundness of his understanding and the candor and openness of his disposition. I have not yet been so fortunate as to discover any very significant mistakes in the reasonings delivered in the preceding volumes, except on one article. But I have found by experience that some of my expressions have not been so well chosen as to guard against all mistakes in the readers; and it is chiefly to remedy this defect that I have subjoined the following appendix.

<meta>This page continues the Appendix started on page 283, where the author discusses his intention to correct and clarify his reasoning on human understanding. Here, he begins to examine the specific nature of belief as it relates to cause and effect.</meta>

### A P P E N D I X.

<margin>collateral</margin>
We can never be induced to believe any matter of fact, except where its cause, or <insert>director</insert> its effect, is present to us; but what the nature is of that belief, which arises from the relation of cause and effect, few have had the curiosity to ask themselves. In my opinion, this dilemma is inevitable. Either the belief is some new idea, such as that of *reality* or *existence*, which we join to the simple conception of an object, or it is merely a peculiar *feeling* or *sentiment*. That it is not a new idea, annexed to the simple conception, may be evinced from these two arguments. *First*, We have no abstract idea of existence, distinguishable and separable from the idea of particular objects. It is impossible, therefore, that this idea of existence can be annexed to the idea of any object, or form the difference between a simple conception and belief. *Secondly*, The mind has the command over all its ideas, and can separate, unite, mix, and vary them, as it pleases; so that if belief consisted merely in a new idea, annexed to the conception, it would be in a man's power to believe what he pleased. We may, therefore, conclude, that belief consists merely in a certain feeling or sentiment; in something, that de-
<meta>catchword: pends</meta>

<summary>The author argues that belief is not a separate idea added to a thought, but rather a distinct feeling or sentiment. He supports this by noting that existence is inseparable from the idea of an object and that belief is not subject to the mind's voluntary control over its ideas.</summary>
<keywords>belief, cause and effect, existence, sentiment, conception, abstract ideas, human nature, philosophy of mind</keywords>

<page-num>285</page-num>
### APPENDIX.

depends not on the will, but must arise from certain determinate causes and principles, of which we are not masters. When we are convinced of any matter of fact, we do nothing but conceive it, along with a certain feeling, different from what attends the mere *reveries* of the imagination. And when we express our incredulity concerning any fact, we mean that the arguments for the fact do not produce that feeling. If belief did not consist in a sentiment different from our mere conception, whatever objects were presented by the wildest imagination would be on an equal footing with the most established truths founded on history and experience. There is nothing but the feeling, or sentiment, to distinguish the one from the other.

THIS, therefore, being regarded as an undoubted truth, *that belief is nothing but a peculiar feeling, different from the simple conception*, the next question that naturally occurs is, *what is the nature of this feeling, or sentiment, and whether it is analogous to any other sentiment of the human mind?* This question is important. For if it is not analogous to any other sentiment, we must despair of explaining its causes, and must consider it as an original principle of the human mind.

<header>A P P E N D I X.</header>

mind. If it is analogous, we may hope to explain its causes through analogy and trace it back to more general principles. Now, everyone will readily admit that there is a greater firmness and solidity in the conceptions that are the objects of conviction and assurance than in the loose and idle daydreams of a castle-builder. They strike us with more force; they are more present to us; the mind has a firmer hold of them and is more influenced and moved by them. It acquiesces in them and, in a sense, fixes and rests itself upon them. In short, they approach closer to the impressions that are immediately present to us, and are therefore analogous to many other operations of the mind.

THERE is not, in my opinion, any possibility of evading this conclusion except by asserting that belief, besides the simple conception, consists of some impression or feeling that is distinguishable from the conception. It does not modify the conception and make it more present and intense; it is only attached to it in the same way that *will* and *desire* are attached to particular conceptions of good and pleasure. All men have always allowed reasoning to be merely an operation of our thoughts or ideas; and however those ideas may be varied to the feeling, nothing ever enters into our *conclusions* but ideas, or our fainter conceptions. For instance; I hear at present a person's voice with whom I am acquainted, and this sound comes from the next room. This impression of my senses immediately conveys my thoughts to the person, along with all the surrounding objects. I picture them to myself as existing at present, with the same qualities and relations that I formerly knew them to possess. These ideas take faster hold of my mind than the ideas of an enchanted castle. They are different to the feeling; but there is no distinct or separate impression attending them. It is the same case when I recollect the several incidents of a journey, or the events of any history. Every particular fact is there the object of belief. occasion, it is when the mind has been agitated with doubts and difficulties; and afterwards, upon taking the object in a new point of view, or being presented with a new argument, fixes and reposes itself in one settled conclusion and belief. In this case there is a feeling distinct and separate from the conception. The passage from doubt and agitation to tranquillity and repose, conveys a satisfaction and pleasure to the mind. But take any other case. Suppose I see the legs and thighs of a person in motion, while some interposed object conceals the rest of his body. Here it is certain, the imagination spreads out the whole figure. I give him a head and shoulders, and breast and neck. These members I conceive and believe him to be possessed of. Nothing can be more evident, than that this whole operation is performed by the thought or imagination alone. The transition is immediate. The ideas strike us immediately. Their customary connection with the present impression, varies them and modifies them in a certain manner, but produces no act of the mind, distinct from this peculiarity of conception. Let anyone examine his own mind, and he will evidently find this to be the truth.

*Secondly*, whatever the case may be regarding this distinct impression, it must be allowed that the mind has a firmer hold, or a steadier conception, of what it takes to be a matter of fact than of fictions. Why then look any further, or multiply suppositions without necessity?

*Thirdly*, we can explain the *causes* of the firm conception, but not those of any separate impression. And not only so, but the causes of the firm conception exhaust the whole subject, and nothing is left to produce any other effect. An inference concerning a matter of fact is nothing but the idea of an object that <insert>has been</insert> frequently conjoined or is associated with a present impression. This is the whole of it. Every part is required to explain, by analogy, the steadier conception; and nothing remains capable of producing any distinct impression.

*Fourthly*, the *effects* of belief in influencing the passions and imagination can all be explained by the firm conception; and there is no need to have recourse to any other principle. These arguments, along with many others listed in the preceding volumes, sufficiently prove that belief only modifies the idea or conception, and renders

Thus, upon a general view of the subject, there appear to be two questions of importance which we may venture to recommend to the consideration of philosophers: *Whether there is anything to distinguish belief from a simple conception besides the feeling or sentiment?* And, *Whether this feeling is anything but a firmer conception, or a firmer hold, that we take of the object?*

If, upon impartial inquiry, the same conclusion that I have formed is assented to by philosophers, the next task is to examine the analogy that exists between belief and other acts of the mind, and to find the cause of the firmness and strength of conception; and I do not consider this a difficult task. The transition from a present impression always enlivens and strengthens any idea. When any object is presented, the idea of its usual attendant immediately strikes us as something real and solid. It is *felt* rather than conceived, and approaches the impression from which it is derived in its force and influence. This I have proved at length. I cannot add any new arguments, though perhaps my reasoning on this whole question concerning cause and effect would

IT frequently happens that when two men have been engaged in any scene of action, one remembers it much better than the other and has all the difficulty in the world making his companion recollect it. He runs over several circumstances in vain; he mentions the time, the place, the company, what was said, and what was done on all sides, until at last he hits on some lucky circumstance that revives the whole and gives his friend a perfect memory of everything. Here, the person who forgets receives at first all the ideas from the discourse of the other, with the same circumstances of time and place; though he considers them as mere fictions of the imagination. But as soon as the circumstance that touches the memory is mentioned, the very same ideas now appear in a new light, and have,

loss for terms to express my meaning. I conclude, by an induction which seems very evident to me, that an opinion or belief is nothing but an idea that is different from a fiction, not in its nature or the order of its parts, but in the *manner* of its being conceived. But when I would explain this *manner*, I find hardly any word that fully fits the case, but am obliged to have recourse to everyone's feeling in order to give them a perfect notion of this operation of the mind. An idea assented to *feels* different from a fictitious idea that the fancy alone presents to us. And this different feeling I endeavor to explain by calling it a superior force, or vivacity, or solidity, or firmness, or steadiness. This variety of terms, which may seem so unphilosophical, is intended only to express that act of the mind which renders realities more present to us than fictions, causes them to weigh more in our thoughts, and gives them a superior influence on the passions and imagination. Provided we agree about the thing, it is needless to dispute about the terms. The imagination has command over all its ideas and can join, mix, and vary them in all possible ways. It may conceive objects with all the circumstances of place and time. It may set them,

<summary>Hume concludes that belief is not a distinct type of idea, but rather a specific "manner" of conceiving an idea that gives it more force, vivacity, and influence than a mere fiction. He argues that while this feeling is difficult to name precisely, it is what allows the mind to distinguish reality from imagination.</summary>
<keywords>belief, induction, opinion, fiction, vivacity, solidity, imagination, mental operations, David Hume</keywords>

Such is the power of suggestion in places, that it is not without reason that the training of memory is derived from them.* Cicero, *De Finibus*, book 5.

# A P P E N D I X.

fictions are connected with nothing that is real. This observation makes us only lend ourselves, so to speak, to the fiction, but it causes the idea to feel very different from the stable, established persuasions founded on memory and custom. They are somewhat of the same kind, but the one is much inferior to the other, both in its causes and effects.

A similar reflection on *general rules* keeps us from increasing our belief upon every increase of the force and vivacity of our ideas. Where an opinion admits of no doubt or opposing probability, we attribute to it a full conviction, even though the lack of resemblance or contiguity may render its force inferior to that of other opinions. It is in this way that the understanding corrects the appearances of the senses and makes us imagine that an object at twenty feet distance seems, even to the eye, as large as one of the same dimensions at ten feet.

<meta>This page continues Hume’s critique of the idea of "power" or "energy." Specifically, it addresses the argument that we derive our idea of power from the internal experience of the will’s command over our bodies and minds.</meta>

<language>English</language>

<page-num>299</page-num>
<header># A P P E N D I X.</header>
<sig>I</sig>
<meta>catchword: can</meta>

that, having in this manner acquired the idea of power, we transfer that quality to matter, where we are not able immediately to discover it. The motions of our body, and the thoughts and sentiments of our mind (they say), obey the will; nor do we seek any further to acquire a just notion of force or power. But to convince us how fallacious this reasoning is, we need only consider that the will, being considered here as a cause, has no more of a discoverable connection with its effects than any material cause has with its proper effect. So far from perceiving the connection between an act of volition and a motion of the body, it is admitted that no effect is more inexplicable from the powers and essence of thought and matter. Nor is the command of the will over our mind any more intelligible. The effect there is distinguishable and separable from the cause, and could not be foreseen without the experience of their constant conjunction. We have command over our mind to a certain degree, but beyond *that*, we lose all dominion over it; and it is evidently impossible to fix any precise bounds to our authority where we do not consult experience. In short, the actions of the mind are, in this respect, the same as those of matter. We perceive only their constant conjunction; nor

I HAD entertained some hopes that, however deficient our theory of the intellectual world might be, it would be free from those contradictions and absurdities which seem to attend every explanation that human reason can give of the material world. But upon a more strict review of the section concerning *personal identity*, I find myself involved in such a labyrinth that, I must confess, I neither know how to correct my former opinions nor how to render them consistent. <meta>This page continues the Appendix where Hume expresses his skepticism and "diffidence" regarding his previous theories. He begins a systematic review of the arguments that led him to doubt the existence of a simple, unified "self."</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>appendix</page-type>
<page-num>301</page-num>
<header># A P P E N D I X.</header>

all my decisions. I shall propose the arguments on both sides, beginning with those that induced me to deny the strict and proper identity and simplicity of a self or thinking being.

**When** we talk of *self* or *substance*, we must have an idea annexed to these terms; otherwise, they are altogether unintelligible. Every idea is derived from preceding impressions; and we have no impression of self or substance as something simple and individual. We have, therefore, no idea of them in that sense.

**Whatever** is distinct is distinguishable; and whatever is distinguishable is separable by thought or imagination. All perceptions are distinct. They are, therefore, distinguishable and separable, and may be conceived as separately existent, and may exist separately, without any contradiction or absurdity.

**When** I view this table and that chimney, nothing is present to me but particular perceptions, which are of a similar nature to all other perceptions. This is the doctrine of philosophers. But this table, which is present to me, and that chimney, may and do exist separately. This is the doctrine of the common people, and implies no contradiction. There is no contradiction, therefore,

<meta>catchword: therefore,</meta>

<summary>Hume argues that the concepts of "self" and "substance" are unintelligible because we have no specific impressions of them as simple or unified things. He asserts that all perceptions are distinct and can be conceived as existing independently of one another.</summary>
<keywords>self, substance, impressions, perceptions, identity, simplicity, philosophers, common people, separability</keywords>

--- Page 310 ---
<meta>This page continues Hume's argument from the previous page, specifically applying the logic used for external objects to the internal "self."</meta>

<page-type>appendix</page-type>
<page-num>302</page-num>
# A P P E N D I X.

therefore, in extending the same doctrine to all perceptions.

**In** general, the following reasoning seems satisfactory. All ideas are borrowed from preceding perceptions. Our ideas of objects, therefore, are derived from that source. Consequently, no proposition can be intelligible or consistent regarding objects which is not also so regarding perceptions. But it is intelligible and consistent to say that objects exist as distinct and independent, without any common *simple* substance or subject of <term>inhesion</term> <note>the state of existing within something else as an attribute or property</note>. This proposition, therefore, can never be absurd regarding perceptions.

**When** I turn my reflection toward *myself*, I can never perceive this *self* without one or more perceptions; nor can I ever perceive anything but the perceptions. It is the composition of these, therefore, which forms the self.

**We** can conceive of a thinking being having either many or few perceptions. Suppose the mind to be reduced even below the life of an oyster. Suppose it to have only one perception, such as thirst or hunger. Consider it in that situation. Do you conceive of anything but merely that perception? Do you have any notion of *self* or *substance*? If not, the

<meta>catchword: addition</meta>

<summary>Hume argues that because we can conceive of objects existing independently without a shared substance, we must also be able to conceive of perceptions as independent. He concludes that the "self" is nothing more than a composition of various perceptions rather than a single, simple substance.</summary>
<keywords>perceptions, ideas, objects, simple substance, inhesion, self, thinking being, Hume, skepticism</keywords>

<page-num>303</page-num>
# A P P E N D I X.

addition of other perceptions can never give you that notion.

**The** annihilation which some people suppose to follow death, and which entirely destroys this self, is nothing but an extinction of all particular perceptions: love and hatred, pain and pleasure, thought and sensation. These, therefore, must be the same as the self, since the one cannot survive the other.

**Is** *self* the same as *substance*? If it is, how can that question arise concerning the subsistence of *self* during a change of *substance*? If they are distinct, what is the difference between them? For my part, I have a notion of neither when they are conceived as distinct from particular perceptions.

**Philosophers** are beginning to be reconciled to the principle *that we have no idea of external substance distinct from the ideas of particular qualities*. This must pave the way for a similar principle regarding the mind: *that we have no notion of it distinct from particular perceptions*.

**So** far, I seem to be supported by sufficient evidence. But having thus loosened all our particular perceptions, when <gloss>a</gloss> I proceed to explain the principle of connection which binds

<page-type>appendix</page-type>
<page-num>304</page-num>
# *A P P E N D I X.*

binds them together, and makes us attribute to them a real simplicity and identity; I am aware that my account is very defective, and that nothing but the seeming evidence of the preceding reasonings could have induced me to accept it. If perceptions are distinct existences, they form a whole only by being connected together. But no connections among distinct existences are ever discoverable by human understanding. We only *feel* a connection or determination <margin>a</margin> of the thought to pass from one object to another. It follows, therefore, that the thought alone finds personal identity when, reflecting on the train of past perceptions that compose a mind, the ideas of them are felt to be connected together and naturally introduce each other. However extraordinary this conclusion may seem, it need not surprise us. Most philosophers seem inclined to think that personal identity *arises* from consciousness; and consciousness is nothing but a reflected thought or perception. The current philosophy, therefore, has so far a promising aspect. But all my hopes vanish when I come to explain the principles that unite our successive perceptions in our thought or consciousness. I cannot discover any theory which gives me satisfaction on this point,

<meta>This page continues Hume’s discussion of the principles of the mind, specifically focusing on the contradiction between distinct perceptions and the lack of a known connection between them.</meta>

<page-type>appendix</page-type>
<page-num>305</page-num>
<header>A P P E N D I X.</header>

In short, there are two principles which I cannot make consistent, nor is it in my power to reject either of them: namely, *that all our distinct perceptions are distinct existences, and that the mind never perceives any real connection among distinct existences.* If our perceptions either resided in something simple and individual, or if the mind perceived some real connection among them, there would be no difficulty in the case. For my part, I must plead the privilege of a skeptic and confess that this difficulty is too hard for my understanding. I do not claim, however, to declare it absolutely insurmountable. Others, perhaps, or I myself, upon more mature reflection, may discover some hypothesis that will reconcile these contradictions.

I shall also take this opportunity to confess two other errors of less importance, which more mature reflection has revealed to me in my reasoning. The first may be found in Volume 1, page 107, where I say that the distance between two bodies is known, among other things, by the angles which the rays of light flowing from the bodies make with each other. distinction. It is the same case with particular sounds, tastes, and smells. These allow for infinite resemblances in their general appearance and comparison, without having any identical common circumstance. We may be certain of this even from the abstract term <term>simple idea</term>. This term encompasses all simple ideas. These resemble each other in their simplicity. And yet, due to their very nature—which excludes all composition—this circumstance, in which they resemble one another, is neither distinguishable nor separable from the rest. It is the same case with all degrees of any quality. They all resemble one another, and yet the quality in any individual instance is not distinct from its degree.

Whichever side mathematicians turn toward, this dilemma still faces them. If they judge equality, or any other proportion, by the accurate and exact standard—namely, the counting of the minute indivisible parts—they both employ a standard that is useless in practice and actually establish the indivisibility of extension, which they endeavor to disprove. Or if they employ, as is usual, the inaccurate standard derived from a comparison of objects based on their general appearance, corrected by measurement and juxtaposition, then their first principles—though certain and infallible—are too coarse to support the subtle inferences they commonly draw from them. These first principles are founded on the imagination and the senses. The

<summary>Hume argues that the only useful concept of equality comes from comparing the appearance of objects. He presents a dilemma for mathematicians, who must choose between an impractical standard of indivisible parts or a sensory standard that is too imprecise for complex mathematical inferences.</summary>

<header>A P P E N D I X.</header>

The conclusion, therefore, can never go beyond, much less contradict, these faculties.

*A note for Volume 1, page 118, line 8, regarding these words (impressions and ideas).*

As long as we limit our speculations to the appearances of objects to our senses, without entering into detailed inquiries concerning their real nature and operations, we are safe from all difficulties and can never be embarrassed by any question. Thus, if it is asked <margin>whether</margin> the invisible and intangible distance placed between two objects is something or nothing, it is easy to answer that it is *something*—namely, a property of the objects which affects the senses in a particular way. If it is asked whether two objects with such a distance between them touch or not, it may be answered that this depends upon the definition of the word *touch*. If objects are said to touch when there is nothing perceptible placed between them, these objects touch. If objects are said to touch when their *images* strike adjacent parts of the eye, and when the hand *feels* both objects one after the other without any intervening motion, these objects do not touch. The appearances of objects to our senses are all

<meta>catchword: all</meta>
<vocab>speculations, appearances, objects, senses, disquisitions, distance, touch, images, feels</vocab>

<summary>Hume argues that philosophical confusion regarding space and contact is avoided by focusing on sensory appearances rather than the ultimate nature of objects. He demonstrates that whether objects are considered to be "touching" depends entirely on whether one defines the term by the lack of visible space or by the lack of sensory motion between them.</summary>
<keywords>perception, senses, distance, touch, impressions, ideas, David Hume, appearance vs reality, spatial extension</keywords>

--- Page 318 ---
<meta>This page completes the discussion from page 309, which argued that philosophical disputes regarding sensory perception often stem from the definitions of the terms used rather than the nature of the objects themselves.</meta>

<page-num>310</page-num>
<header>A P P E N D I X.</header>

all consistent; and no difficulties can ever arise, except from the obscurity of the terms we use.

If we carry our inquiry beyond the appearances of objects to the senses, I am afraid that most of our conclusions will be full of skepticism and uncertainty. Thus, if it is asked whether or not the invisible and intangible distance is always full of *body*, or of something that—by an improvement of our organs—might become visible or tangible, I must acknowledge that I find no very decisive arguments on either side; though I am inclined toward the contrary opinion, as being more suitable to common and popular notions. If the *Newtonian* philosophy is rightly understood, it will be found to mean no more. A vacuum is asserted: That is, bodies are said to be placed in such a manner as to receive other bodies between them, without impulsion or penetration. The real nature of this position of bodies is unknown. We are only acquainted with its effects on the senses and its power of receiving body. Nothing is more suitable to that philosophy than a modest skepticism to a certain degree, and a fair confession of ignorance in subjects that exceed all human capacity.

->F I<-

<summary>Hume concludes that philosophical difficulties arise primarily from obscure terminology and argues that human inquiry should remain skeptical when moving beyond sensory appearances. He suggests that Newtonian concepts like the vacuum simply describe the arrangement of bodies rather than their ultimate, unknown nature.</summary>
<keywords>Newtonian philosophy, vacuum, skepticism, sensory perception, body, matter, human capacity, impulsion, penetration</keywords>
